===== 0002601_19160923_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A Costly Trick. What a lot of people yield to the temptation to attempt to defraud a railway company! Really, to take the lowest ground, the game is not worth the candle. A while ago a chance acquaintance gleefully explained to me a scheme which he had devised for saving a few coppers in this way, "without the slightesf risk of detection," and on the very next Saturday he was caught in the act, and, besides suffering the humiliation of exposure, had £3 to pay in fine and costs.

Another Dodge. Once I saw a very well-known resident in an East Lancashire town enter a first-class com- partment with a person of the Opposite sex at Bradford. -I was travelling by the same train, and noticed that after riding some 20 miles they took advantage of a stop to alight and saunter about the platform. They completed their journey in a third-class carriage, and I after- wards ascertained from ,a collector that they simply held third-class returns from that point. A few weeks later, this gentleman over- took me in the street, and, clapping me on the shoulder, gave me a boisterous greeting. He proceeded to indulge in a little chaff, but when I asked him if he had been to Bradford lately, he gravely declared that it was a place he very seldom visited. I then told him that not long before I had seen a prominent citizen enter a first-class carriage at Bradford with a lady whom I did not know, and that the pair changed to the inferior class at a station far down the line for which they had merely return tickets for their destination. Remarking that it was a cloudy evening, the fellow hurriedly left me, and he always avoided me after that.

" Excluded and Isolated." I have read with interest the recent state- ments respecting "the position of Cairo-street Chapel and its minister." It is an Old contro- versy. In one town in which I lived some years, the committee. of the Sunday School Union decided by a fair majority to admit the Unitarians, whereupon the Wesleyan repre- sentatives withdrew, subsequently forming- a circuit union of their own. On the constitution of a Free Church Council in that district, the Unitarians made a grievance of the fact that they were not invited to join, whereupon the promoters of the movement pointed out that they were affiliated with the National " Evangelical " Free Church Council, and had therefore no power to include Unitarians. The Unitarian Position.

Our Unitarian friends always expect and pre- pare for this sort of thing. I ?ecall a confer- ence of the Yorkshire Union of Unitarian and Free Christian Churches at Huddersfield some years ago. A minister from Southport spoke on " The good and evil of denominations." His main argument was that the so-called schisms were needed, and had strengthened the spiritual life of the nation. Toward the conclusion he said he could see very few signs that the Free Churches would welcome the Unitarian and Free Christian Churches into their fold. The Unitarian Churches, he added, were the Protestants amongst the Protestants, and in all probability would still have to carve out their own path.

More About Wasps. Last week's item headed "Drunkenness among wasps" constrains me to set down a few simple observations of my own which have led me to conclude that the wasp is a lazy, cowardly bully. Wasps would be quite service- able if it were possible to keep them to their own proper vocation, but as soon as there are signs of an easy, comfortable living without exertion, they become hopelessly demoralised. Does the housewife set on fruit to boil, they scent their "favourite tipple from afar, and become a nuisance in a neighbourhood where perhaps none of their kind have previously been seen during the season—unless, indeed, it were in the confectionery and sweatshop windows. This I have repeatedly demonstrated: however valiant, to all appearance, and vicious in the use of its sting, 'the wasp is, feally a "pusillanimous critter." If you can give one ever such a slight tap with any light article you may, happen to haveliandy, it - will, im- mediately grovel at your feet. I wonder if wasps have a weakness for scent among other luxuries—say, with a preference for lavender. This latter was used by a lady member of a party I fell in with on Saturday afternoon. The wasps were rather numerous, yet they troubled none but the lavender-scented one—not even when we attacked them with our walking sticks. The. lavender had a strange fascination for them, and they fell an easy prey to the lady's defenders. The Lady Motorist.

As to that bit of " Gossip " respecting the lady taxi-cab driver, a doctor with a consider- able practice told me the other day that, th war having deprived him of his male chauffeur, he had had one of his housemaids trained to drive, and she had turned out "a gem." She cleaned and prepared the car herself and handled it to perfection, and in the early morn- ing and at thE, close of the day would expend her superfluous energy in the garden with the aid of the lawnmower or in householl duties demanding vigorous exercise. In a word, she was "keen as mustard" on work. At this rate, mere man will have to look to his laurels. Ah, but is he not adding to them by strenuous exertions in a more dangerous sphere? Blinded Heroes.

Again what of the blinded sailors and soldiers in whose interests last night's concert was held in the Parr Hall? , Helen Keller's argument that mach of the prevalent blindness is un- necessary and avoidable hardly applies here. Among other things, these dear fellows will have to learn to read all over again. Would that all the blind had the advantages of a common print, for, strange as it may seem, it is not so at present. Some time ago I read in an article in "World's Work" that there is one insti- tution in the United States using embossed characters (line-letter), in spite of the fact that most of the blind prefer a point system; another, American braille (raised dots); another, New York Point—a kind of braille, in which a new periodical, the " Matilda Ziegler Magazine l'or the Blind," "the boon ifor which we have waited for many years," Pis printed. To print this periodical at all is expensive; it is much more expensive now that it has to be done in so many different types. "A plague 'upon all these prints!" exclaimed the writer. "Let us have one system, whether it is an ideal one or not. For my part, I wish nothing had been in- vented except European braille." " Cruel Rumour,"

The bit of good news under this heading re- minded me of an incident occurring on the eve of the summer holidays. A hardworking man and his wife were at the point of departure for a few days' rest and change down by the sea, when a fussy neighbour rushed up on the plat- form and knked if they had not heard that their soldier-son was reported, missing. They had good reason to doubt the evil tidings; still, while deciding not to allow the rumour to inter- fere with their programme—a resolute step which was fully justified, as the sequel proved —they were naturally somewhat perturbed until fully assured that it was baseless. Most of us fail to understand, and certainly do not, envy, the state .of mind of those who are capable of deriving amusement from this kind of "joke."

Spelling. Last week's little story of the smart child who complained that her spelling book was "all wrong," reminded me once more of the notice " Ile soled hear," to be seen not so long ago in the window of a small Warrington -shop—sug- gesting that the old spelling bee, which was a popular form of entertainment SO to 40 years ago, might not unprofitably be 'rived. -

The Only Way. Teresa: "The man I marry must be bold and fearless." Viola: "Yes, dear; he must."

===== 0002601_19160923_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
lib

CONTENDING ATOM.

Alderman Bennett On The Mobilisation Of Intellect.

ADDRESS TO BRITISH ASSOCIATION.

Alderman Arthur Bennett read a paper on "The Federation of Cognate Societies" at the recent Conference of the Corresponding locieties of the British Association at New- castle-on-Tyne.

Federation, he explained, he had defined as "union for common purposes, liberty in mat- ters of separate concern," which, in the enthusiasm of youth, he had trielcl to prove was an 'idea that, properly interpreted, would not only solve the Irish question and pave the way to a really united Empire, but would lead by stages to Tennyson's sublime ideal, "the Parliament of man, the federation of the world." We had seen the principle applied with great success in Australia and ,South Africa, and found men of every party feeling towards the simple truth that federation is the only way to solve the riddle of these islands consistently with the satisfaction of the claims of various parts of them to what is popularly called Home Rule. The Nonconformist bodies of the country have been drawn closer together through the medium of a Free Church Council and are rapidly advancing towards still closer union on the same elastic lines. Capital and Labour are largely organising on these lines, and the idea had spread, almost unconsciously, to almost ever, field of human activity. A Warrington Federation.

In 1905 he advocated liefore the Warrington Literary and Philosophical Society and pleaded for a federation of the local societies interested in intellectual pursuits, with head- quarters at the Old Academy. He admitted the difficulties of merging these societies, but they could federate by having a general Coun- cil to regulate procedure, avoid overlapping and clashing of dates, secure an interchange of lectures and arrange occasional joint meet- ings. In May, 1914, his scheme was disinterred and at a meeting of the principal societies in the town the proposal was approved and a committee appointed to prepare a definite Scheme. That scheme was submitted in June, 1914, to the delegates of thirteen societies, representing practically every haven of local intellectual activity, and it was decided that the Federation be formed upon lines which were ordered to be submitted to the societies, with a request to them to send delegates to a meeting of the Federation. And "after that the Deluge." This frightful war broke upon us like a storm of Hell before the project had matured, and, like many other, hopeful and progressive movements, it is still in abeyance. '

Why Not Nationally?

If thie. idea could be applied locally, why not nationally? His object was the gradual mobilisation of all the intellectual forces of the country, and surely the need was never more acute than nosy. We are, or have been, one of the worst organised countries in the world; those who clamoured for reform had been as voices crying in the wilderness; the lesson had to be written in lightning before our slumbrous realm would learn. Instead of developing into an ordered commonwealth we had degenerated into a fortuitous concourse of contending atoms. Our .politics had become a mere scramble for the loaves and fishes.; our 'Churches wrangled over things irrelevant and overlapped and competed at every turn; our industries were animated mainly by the greed of gain and those of them that needed extra brains, newer methods and better scientific skill were quietly appropriated by more enter- prising lands. Instead of combining to make the most of their common country, the classes and the masses stood aloof and glared at each other and ever and anon growled like, two packs of hungry wolves. Although we; were spending more on education than ever before nobody- was really educated. Our Empire, with all its greatness, latent loyalty, untold possibilities in men and material, was such a ramshackle arrangement that one cannot won- der at the Germans for thinking that when the grim hour struck it would' incontinently fall to pieces. As for the world, the beet it could do after an infancy of unimaginable years was to use its ever-growing powers for purposes of sheer destruction, "plot mutual slaughter" and "reel back into the beast"

===== 0002601_19160923_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
A Vision of Better Things.

But amid the chaos there had been the vision of "men with growing wings." This welter of blood and sorrow has revealed such heights of human nobleness as we had never dreamed of; such possibilities of properly co- ordinated effort, turned to less ignoble uses, as the bravest hitherto had scarcely ventured to conceive.

The different societies in Warrington at pre- sent meet in different places—ranging from "pubs." to clubs and from Masonic Halls to Church Parlours. A federation might secure or even build in every town appropriate pre- mises, fitted with the requisite appliances and supplied with smaller rooms, which was quite beyond the power of separate societies. District federations would be a natural corollary to town federations, and these in turn would be linked up with the central Qrganisation, national, imperial or cosmopolitan, as the case might be. If England organised and really rose to the height of her opportunities there was not any nation in the world apable of greater things. On the motion of the Alderman the following resolution was unanimously carried : "That the committee of the conference of delegates be requested, to recommend the various constituent societies to consider the desirability of forming local and national federations of societies with kindred .aims."

FOR CONSCIENCE' SAKE.

"Is there anything further about the two. Penketh conscientious objectors and the pay- ment of poor relief?" asked the Rev. A. M. Mitchell at the Board of Guardians on Wed- nesday. The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) : I have car- ried out the Board's instructions and had inquiries made. The Rey. A. M. Mitchell: Has it been found necessary to grant/ relief to any of them? The Clerk: No. Both women have definitely stated that they will not have poor relief or relief from the Government unless their hus- bands are working and earning it. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell : That is very nice and noble of them. It is quite evident that the Tribunals have not treated conscientious objectors as they should have been, and am quite sure the Guardians would do all they I could for their wives and families.

'NOBLE AND STRAIGHTFORWARD.'

Butcher Congratulated By Board Of Guardians.

some time ago Mr. H. Singleton, butcher;was granted by the Board of Guardians an increase of 3d. per lb. over contract price owing to the. enhanced cost of meat. On Wednesday', atl a meeting of the Guardians, Mr. A. Bottomley (the clerk) reported that Mr. Singleton intended lowering the price by a penny per lb. as meat had become slightlk cheaper. "Good !" exclaimed several of the members. Mr. W. C. Brooks: It is very nice for mem- bers to say "Good J"—and it is good—but, a. ther; is not a contractor in the whole universe who has done a similar thing, I think we ought to send a letter of our appreciation of his noble and straightforward conduct,-

Mr. W.J. Shaw: I quite agree. The Rev. A(M. Mitchell: Do you think that is a wise thing to do? Mr. Shaw: It is wise to accept the penny.

The Chairman: Mr. Mitchell only objects on a point of principle. But it is rather unique that a contractor should do this. The reverend gentleman agreed that the contractor might be told that the matter had been reported to the Board. Mr. T. Dale: I think we might appreciate what he has done, but not applaud it. It was decided that a letter of appieciation and thanks should be sent.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 23, 1916.

CLEVER STUDENTS

Heartily Congratulated By The

Education Committee.

The Education Committee, on Monday, de- voted a considerable time to congratulatirig Mr. Leslie John Witts and Miss Kathleen Brown on winning the Warrington Scholarship, which entitles them to a three years' course at Man- chester University. Two other competitors, Miss Ethel White Johnson and Miss Bessie Lee "(candidates for the teaching profession) were awarded £25 each for four years, conditionally upon their taking a Degree course. The enconiums of •the committee regarding these two ladies were phrased, with equal heartiness and sincerity. Mr. John Upson made an explanation re- garding the proposed grant to Misses Johnson and Lee. He said the committee did not always get a supply of students who were competent to pass the examination, and to qualify -for the scholarship. Last year they did not award the scholarship at all, because there were no suit- able candidates. This year quite a number sat, and three girls passed the examination,.receiv- ing high approval from the authorities. While the committee had only one scholarship at their disposal, they did not think the other two girls should lose the opportunity of a university training. In view of the fact that they did not award the scholarship last year, they thought they would be acting well within their authority by making the proposed grants. Had the first girl, who did remarkably well, not been competing, either of the _other two would have gained the scholarship, and the School Management Committee thought they were well justified in suggesting the course they had done. Alderman Bennett particularly welcomed the suggestion of the sub-committee. No work which the Education Committee could do was of more value than that of affording' an oppor- tunity to really qualified boys and girls to take advantage of those higher educational facilities which in the ordinary course might not be available. He complimented the four students and, knowing Witts, he declared his belief that he would go far in the educational world, and it was a pleasure to be able to give him a help- ing hand. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Joseph asked whether the two girls who had come so near success were disqualified from sitting a second time for the scholarship? Mr. Upton replied that they were. He said that with some reluctance, because it -was opposed to his personal view of the matter, but he must be loyal td the committee. The majority thought that as the girls had already had one try and had also accepted a student teachership which carried a monetary payment of £2O, they would be in too favourable a position to compete against girls whop were a year younger. _ _ _

- Mr: A. J. Jolley: I think Ethel Johnson can sit again, but the other girl cannot. Mr. Upson said he was under the impression that, as both. were candidates for the teaching profession, they would be debarred. Mr. Jolley: One is a student teacher and the other is not. If they were both student teachers they would both be disqualified. The Chairman (Councillor H. Roberts) first offered the congratulations of the com- mittee to Mr. William E. Mason, the first winner of a Warrington scholarship, on his con- tinued progress, and added that if ever a 'scholarship had been justified by results it had been in that case. The places of Leslie J. Witts and Kath- leen Brown, the two new scholarship holders, were simply excellent in the examination, as also were those of Ethel W. Johnson and Bessie' Lee. If the committee had only worded their scheme a little differently so that they' could have picked up in any year the scholarships which they did not award when they had not candidates of sufficient excellence, all these four might possibly have held scholarships. He also congratulated Mr. Thomas Malkin, a student at the Technical Institute, who had secured top. place in the examination in chemistry of the Lancashire and Cheshire ,Union of Institutes. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19160923_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
WANTED, A BENEFACTOR. How Secondary Education Could Make Progress.

The Secondary School was awarded favour- able mention by the Education Committee on Monday evening, and, in order that, its benefits to the community might be-extended, Mr. John Upson, realising the impossibility at the pre- sent time of utilising ratepayers' money, sug- gested that some local lady or gentleman might yield up £50,000 for the purpose. It was during the discussion on the annual report of the headmaster ,Of the school (Mr. Broome) that the idea was mooted, *Mr. C. H. Unsworth having asked what it contained, and Mr. Upson replied that it was of a very favour- able character. It was the first report Mr. Broome had presented since his appointment, and the Governors were extremely giatified at the uniform stream of successes, both by past and present students—some of them of a very high order. The committee felt they had done the right thing in appointing Mr. Broome to that very important position. His enthusiasm and his professional skill had so acted upon the students who had come under his charge that they had participated in his enthusiasm, .and had worked strAnuously and with very brilliant results in many directions. The report of 'the head master of the School of Art (which was also submitted to the committee) was not of so satisfactory a nature owing to the falling off in the number of students. That school was under " the very serious consideration of the School ,Management Committee. They were a little perplexed and puzzled as to how things would turn out, but in that case, as in so many others, they 'could" only "wait and see." The School Management Committee, he added, had instructed their Director (Mr. J. Moore Murray) to consult with the architect of the Secondary School as to the possibility of,carry- ing out—in conformity with the present' archi- tectural features—certain alterations in order to provide a head master's room, of which there was a great need, as, at present, what was described as the head master's room was used as a common office and store room. It was desirable that Mr. Broome should have a room in which to interview parents, Inspectors and Governors. The only doubt in the minds of the committee was as to whether the suggested new room at the top of the staircase could- be erected without spoiling the appearance of the building, and on that account they decided to approach the architect. The whole of our secondary education was cramped and hindered by lack of accommodation. It was useless talking, but he wanted the public to know that at the very earliest moment they must, if the Education Committee were to fulfil their duty adequately, find additional accommodation for secondary students. Again the terrible want pf pence tied them up. Wbuld that some' local benefactor would spend £50,000 in the erection of another secondary school or in dupli- cating the present school. He believed the original plans provided for the duplication of the school when the opportunity came—and it had arrived much more quickly than the erectors of the school anticipated. They could , do- nothing while the war continued. But in I the meantime the young people who were I going to be the winners of the war after the I war—the conimercial,and industrial war—were intellectually and professionally., perishing for lack of opportunity to train their mental and moral faculties and powers.

Alderman Bennett expressed his pleasure that the proposed alterations were to conform with the present architectural features. One had had many bitter experiences of attempted en- largements when the previous archi*tural features had been utterly, ignored for the sake of what might seem to be practical utility.

BUTTER OR MARGARINE?

Mr. W. J. Shaw's "Uneasiness" About Workhouse Inmates.

Mr. W. J. Shaw asked at the Board of Guar- dians' meeting on Wednesday what difference there would be if butter were substituted for margarine, as he was told that - margarine should not be used_in that institution.

The Chairman (Mr. A. 3. jOile;)TTl.ie Board has settled that point.

Mr. Shaw: Yes, up to now; but I wish to draw the attention of the Board to the fact that there if an uneasiness outside that butter should be used.

II So saying, he resumed his seat and the "next libusiness" was begun.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT IDEAL.

Faults In Our Educational System.

EFFORTS FOR IMPROVE- MENT.

An important discussion upon the future of eduCation in the borough took place at the monthly meeting of the Education Committee on Monday evening, oOuncillor Henry Roberts presiding. Apart from an allegation, and, perhaps, admission, that our present methods of education are inefficient or inadequate, the regrettableinews that commercial subjects are not to be taught at the Secondary School yet awhile was made known. The discussion , was initiated by Mr. John Upson, who, in moving the minutes of the 'School Management Committee, said that that committee considered the development of the 'commercial side of the Secondary School prior to the war, and a very practical scheme Was evolved by the Director, and approved by the sub-committee. If the war had not inter- vened, the sclieme would no doubt have been in operatio4 now. In the circumstances, ,how- ever, they could not proceed. But without carrying out the full scheme, it was thought that something could be done in the traijning of some of their young people in the Secondary School, who desired to enter upon a commer- cial career. They advertised for a teacher of the necessary subjects, but, unfortunately, they had not been able to secure a suitable man. They had only two applications and they thought it would be better to defer the appointment until there were a greater num- ber of men qualified to take up the work and do it effectively. The question of the commer- cial side of the work of the Secondary School was ever present in the minds of the members of the School Management Committee. They were very anxious to meet the( needs of the community by turning out students who were adequately trained in commercial subjects, but who, in addition, had a good general educa- tion.- That was an ideal which he hoped they would realise in the near future.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
Low Level of Intelligence—

Expressing the opinion that it was disap- pointing, especially at the present juncture, that they should have to defer a branch of education which was so enormously important. and of which they had somewhat lost sight in the past, Alderman Bennett admitted with regret that they could not at present overcome the practical difficulties; .but he hoped the matter would be kept continually before the School Management Committee,- and that they would not be satisfied until they were able to give that commercial education whtch was so vitally necessary in view of the expected developments of our country. He was, how- ever, glad to express his satisfaction that the Rev. Canon Morley Steienson, Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes, the Rev. T. A. Hind, Mr. A. J. Jolley and Mr. John Upson had been appointed a sub-committee for the purpose of considering and eventually reporting to the School Man- agement Committee upon communications from the Association of Education Committees and the''Newport Education Committee with reference to present conditions and important aspects of national education. He said the feeling was very much in the air that, in spite of all the money we had spent on education, we had not yet arrived at the ideal system. From daily experience they found that people who had been through the elementary schools, and in some cases through the secondary schools were absolutely ignor- ant of some of the. things of life which really mattered. They had a smattering of certain definite subjects in- cluded in the curriculum, but once they got outside those they seemed to be wholly lost. The general level of intelligence of children who had passed through the schools of War- rington and elsewhere was remarkably low. He did not want to blame the schools. The fault was perhaps to a large extent rh the pupils themselves, but he could give them staggering illustrations of what he meant. Perhaps the curriculum itself was somewhat to blame. He knew cases of lads and girls, who had had what was regarded as an inferior education, but who, in the general things of life which really mattered, seemed to be more intelligent than those who had gone through a full course of instruction. He did not think the fault was in the teachers, who were very loyal and enthusiastic, -but he hoped that by deliberations on the lines propOsed the com- mittee would arrive at some method by which they could bring out the best in every lad and girl in the country. Endorsing the remarks of Alderman Ben- nett as to the national importance of educa- tion, Councillor Joseph said he hoped :he proposal mentioned would lead to similarly able men in other parts of ,the country taking up the subject and comparing notes so as to arrive -at the best possible solution of such a vital question. —But Sound at Heart.

The Chairman, was rather optimistic, for while he joined with Alderman Bennett in appreciating that there were serious defects, he could not help pointing out that in this time of national and imperial crisis, when the vast mass of the people of the country, whe- ther on the field of battle or in the munition works, were upholding the highest and 'best traditions of our race, there could not be any- thing very seriously add radically wrong with a system of elementary education, poor as it was, which had given us a generation of men and women of the character and quality of whom .we were 'so proud to-day. (Hear, hear.) If, with the imperfect methods which the war had disclosed to be perhaps more in existence than anticipated, men and women had clone so much, what might be• expected when, with a better and higher type of education, they were called upon to bear the responsibilities of Empire? He, was full of confidence and did not think the Sun of the Empire was at all beginning to set, but that in years to come it would shine with greater effulgence than ever. (Applause.) • In his reply Mr. Upson said it was admitted that the ideal had not been reached, but one could parallel the - cases of gross ignorance alluded to by Alderman Bennett with examples on the, other side. , Not long ago he read a letter written by a lad at the _Front just prior to his death . in. action. The lad came out of one of the poorest houses in the bor- ough and left scliool when only, 13 years old In the whole of the, eight pagea he wrote there was no mistake in grammar, spelling, or punctuation. The letter would not have dis- graced the pages of ".The Spectator." Mr Upson went on to argue that those who were carrying on the practical work of education were crippled_and hampered in every conceiv-i able way by the .eternal lack of pence. All the teachers in the service of the committee, he said, had one Object in view—to turn out better citizens' than those who had already left the schools. At the same time, the war had very largely- proved the value of the education which boys and' girls received in elementary schools. (Applause.)

IN THE COURTS.

NO LICENCE.

Fined For Illegally Trading As

Pawnbrokers.

While the maximum penalty for acting as a pawnbroker without a licence is £5O, George Wilcock and his wife, Mary A. Wilcock, con- victed of this offenge at Tuesday's Borough Police Cdurt, were fined only £7 10s. each. The prosecution was brought in the name of George H. Reynolds, excise officer, and Mr. Edwin R. Booth, solicitor, London, appeared for the Customs authorities.

Mr. Wilcock said he ha'd nothing at all to do with the business, and his wife made a special defence.

Mr. Booth said the prosecution/was brought under the Act of 1872; and Section 6 under which the defendants were alleged to come, de- fined the business of a pawnbroker as one who keeps a shop for the taking in of goods or chattels as security for money given in advance thereon not exceeding £lO. Section 37 provided for the payment of a pawnbroker's licence of £7 10s., and the penalty for failing to do so was £5O. He said he would produce as evidence a little girl aged 16 years and a boy aged 11 years, who redeemed a pair of boots on the 11th July for is. 9d. which had been pledged the preceding day for, is. 6d. The police officer who went to defendant's premises would tell the Court that he found the shop full of bundles of goods of all shapes and sizes—two or ;three hundred bundles—and a variety of articles, fiat irons, workmen's cans and baskets, Sm. The female defendant told the officer that she bought goods from customers and sold them baok to them.

A little girl gave evidence as to the transac- tion mentioned. She had been going there for about four years for the same purpose. The little boy said he had taken in bundles for Mrs. Wilcock to lend money on. Twopence interest was paid for every shilling.

Methodical Classification.

• Detective-Sergeant Reddington said he visited the premises on the 14th July, in company with Detective-Sergeant Summers. He had a con- versation with the female defendant in the shop and told her she vvas 'carrying on the business of pawnbroker. She said she was not aware of it, but that she kept goods for customers and sold them back to them. Witness told her that N%76.--s solely the business of a pawnbroker. With reference to cards having the name and amount attached to each article, ,she said she put them there so that she would know what she paid for thetn. She was very sorry that she charged in- terest on the goods, but she had to get a living. In the back part of the shop on shelves he found bundles of every description, and articles of clothing were hanging up. Each had a piece of paper pinned on with the name of the person who brought the goods there and the price given for them. There were flat irons, work- men's cans, everything imaginable on the fiber, with pieces of paper attached. It was purely the business of a pawnbroker. He had known her to be carrying on this business for the past eight ' years. , The shop was carried on as a secondhand business.

Detective-Sergeant Summers, who corrobor- ated, said that there were pieces of paper with the names and prices in the pockets of coats hanging up. Everything was arranged in the most methodical order and all the goods were classified.

Mrs., Wilcock said that these things at the back of the premises were laid by for the people who sold them. She 'sold these children things which she had bought from them. The Chairman (Councillor Henry Roberts) asked defendant if she had bought the things outright what need was there for putting the names,of the people on them: Defendant said she got a paper from the police every morning with -a list of things that had been stolen, and in case' she bought stolen articles she could identify the persons she got them from.

A Previous Charge.

Superintendent Williamson said that in Afay, 1914, accused were • before the Court on a similai charge. On that occasion they were ordered to pay costs and a very strong caution was administered. They then gave an under- taking to desist from the practice. • The Chairman said the defendants had been committing a fraud on the revenue for some considerable time. Each of them must be fined £7 10s., being the amount of the licence they should have paid, or, in default, two months' imprisonment. If they intended to carry on this business they would have to take out the pawnbroker's licence,

DEFENCE OF THE REALM.

Military Service_ Act And Registration

Order Cases.

Several cases under the Military Service Act and Registration Order were heard at the Borough Police Court on Monday. Patrick Cullen, no settled abode, was charged with +failing to report himself to the authorities under the Military Service Act, and, further, with failing to register. Defendant pleaded guilty. Mr. Nicholls, chief constable, said defendant is about 36 years of age. Men of his class .put the military people to no end of trouble, ,and he submitted that he should not be treated the same as a man who flies to the Colours, lie should be punished as well as handed over to the military.—The magistrates ordered defend- ant to be handed over and fined him 405., to be deducted from his Army pay. , Wm. Anderson, no settled abode, stated that he had been registered while an inmate of Swansea Prison, but did not get a card. He said he was 39 years of age, had been medically examined in Liverpool, and was willing to join the Army. On the other hand the Chief Con- stable said the man had served a term in Usk Prison and had been five days in a Welsh /66gi- ment, from Which he was discharged as unfit. Fined 40s. or a month.

Patrick Cooney, 11, Stanley-street, was charged by Detective-Sergeant Reddington, for failing to register.—The Chief Constable said the plan expressed his willingness to join the Army, probably because he knew he was unfit. (Laughter.) Ile had come -over from Ireland about three months ago and had been in War- rington since the 23rd June.—He was fined 205.; or, in default, 14 days' imprisonment, John Cooke, no fixed residence, who stated he had been registered in Shepherd-street, Pres- ton, was fined 405.; or a month's imprisonment in default: The Chief Constable said the man had evidently signed the usual list kept in lodging-houses, as required by he Registration of Aliens' Act.

A fine of 10s., or, in default, 11 days, was imposed on Peter Banbrick, a nomadic Scots- man, who had failed to register. The Chief Constable raised a laugh by saying defendant was the dirtiest Scotsman he had ever seen, and, judging by his nose, he must have been working last in a distillery. Defendant said he worked in the Dunlop Rubber Company. The Chief Constable: Well, I think they use spirit there. (Laughter.) In addition to a charge of being 'drunk and disorderly, an elderly man named William O'Connor,/no settled abode, was prosecuted for not having a registration card. Defendant said that he was serving in the 'Army when the Registration Acrcame into force, and wb's,only discharged on the 7th April of the present year. The Chief Constable said it was true that de- fendant had been in the Army. It appeared that he had served 306 days and was discharged on the 7th April, 1916, as being no longer fit for military service. His age was entered as 44 years, but he (the Chief Constable) supposed it should ,be. 64.

Defendant said he was 59, but had given his age as 44 when he joined. The Chief Constable: That may be so. Defendant was fined 95., or, in default, seven days' imprisonment on the charge of drunken- ness and disorderly conduct.

CHARGE AGAINST ARMY CORPORAL.

At the Borough Police Court, yesterday, Corporal Joseph Thomason, a the South Lan- cashire Regiment, was charged by P.C. Clarke (Cheshire Lines Committee) with having been drunk and disorderly at the Central Railway Station the previous night. He was further charged with assaulting Ticket Collector Har- rington and Police-Constable Joynson. Constable Clarke stated that accused, who, with a private, was escorting a prisoner, was creating a disturbance on the south platform. The clerk in the booking office had refused to issue a ticket as the 10.15 train from Liverpool was about to leave the station. Defendant struck Ticket Collector, Harrington across the face and also assaulted P.C. Joynson in a simi- lar manner. He was in a drunken condition and used filthy and abusive language. He was arrested and taken to the Police Station.

• Corroborative evidence was given by P.C. Joynson, Ticket Collector Harrington, and the booking clerk, Mr. Jackson. Defendant indignantly denied that he was

• drunk or that he had used filthy language. It was not true that he had assaulted the persons mentioned.

Chief Constable Nicholls said that on the pre- vious night at 6.30, the prisoner, with another escort, arrived at the Police Station to take over an Army absentee. When they came they were sober and the absentee was handed over at 6.30. They should have taken the prisoner away by the train before seven. When they turned up again at the Police Station after ten their condition was obvious. It was clear that the prisoner was too drunk to be allowed to go into the train.

Defendant renewed his denial of the charges preferred against him and said he allowed the prisoner to go home to get some clothes and to see his wife and children. They all had some dinner in the house and a drink of beer. He had only one other drink afterwards during the evening. So long as he got his prisoner back at a reasonable time it was all right. He was naturally excited at not being allowed to catch the train.

The magistrates fined defendant five shillings.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
EXCLUDED AND ISOLATED.

The Position Of Cairo Street Chapel

And Its Minister.

Sir,—l feel that some comment must be made on the Rev. W. Reid's very sympathetic and illuminating letter of last week. But, first of all, let me remove what may be a certain dis- agreeable thought in the minds of some of your readers.

It may he imagined that, in the sermon I Preached a fortnight ago, I went out of my way considerably to criticise unfavourably my brother Free Church ministers. What I said Was in explanation of our position to my own People, for our isolation implies condemnation on the part of others, and we must occasionally justify our position for our own satisfaction. Vurther, it is contended by everyone at the pre- sent time that Churches and even the ChriAtian faith are in some peril; that they have to meet a new situation demanding greater loyally and service, and if they do not prepare themselves and reform themselves they are doomed. And, I for one, am convinced that unhappy divisions Is one of the great causes of the ineffectiveness of the whole Church, and that now is the time to examine ourselves, and, if pbasible, draw closer together in united service. So much in justification of this discussion.

Now, Mr. Reid informs us that on two occasions there have been discussions amongst Free Church ministers as to whether the minis- ter of Cairo-street Chapel should be admitted to their fellowship, and that the decisions on both occasions have been that he shalL.not be admitted. To say the least of it, this is not a very dignified position for the oldest Free Church in 'Warrington to ovupy, a Church Which, as I said' a fortnight ago, was establishei in the interests of freedom in worship, and Which has been served by some of the ablest ministers and scholars and most useful and honourable citizens of Warrington; and surely, one can be excused for being indignant at such treatment. It is but necessary to recall the, close connection of The Academy with Cairo- street Chapel, and the names of Priestley, John Howard, Letitia Barbauld, Philip Car- penter, Rev. R. Pilcher, who to his honour and glory was known amongst many as "The Good Samaritan," and orm.wiLl realise that there can be no reasonable ground for this isolation and ostracism. On the contrary, if the Free Church ministers had but a glimmering of "the his- torical sense," and took the trouble to find out how Cairo-street Chapel, has been and is in- volved in the best traditions of the town, they would count it an honour to have it in their fellowship, and to work in harmony with it. However, I do not want to say hard words. But, we are told that "it is from the tail of the sermon there strikes the note which accounts for the undesirable position complained of." It is because of our position about the Trinity. Now, I am not going to enter into a theological discussion at the moment. But, I should like to be quite candid and positive. There is only one doctrine of the Trinity, and it is the basis of all statements of belief of the so-called orthodox Churches. It finds classic and authoritative expression in the Athanasian Creed. For myself, I reject that doctrine as there stated, because—and I say this in all humility and only for myself—it is unintelli- gible, unscriptural, and unsound. There are many Free Church teachers who have evolved doctrines of the Trinity of their own, and with many of them I can agree. There is one in last

week's "Christian Commonwealth." But, when one speaks of the Ancient Catholic doc- trine of the Trinity, it is the one set forth in the Athanasian Creed which Is -._twetint. Now, do these Free Church ministers all agree on this doctrine? Will they all accept Mr. Reid's statement of it? It is no use quibbling about the matter for a moment. I know that they do not; and there are hundreds of Free Church ministers who do not. There are a score of interpretations of the Trinity, and they are all as heretical as my Unitarian doctrine, and some of them a good deal more so. Therefore, the position is this. The minister of Cairo-street Chapel is excluded from Free Church fellowship because he does not agree with these ministers about a doctrine on which they themselves can claim no unanimity. But, there is a more serious ques- tion I should like to ask. If one is excluded from Free Church fellowship because, in the opinion of Free Church ministers, he is unsound on the Trinity, is the same rule enforced with regard to Church members and office bearers? If it is not; and I know it is not, then there is inconsistency which is grossly culpable. But, is it .not time that we, ministers of all Churches, realised that there is a great deal more on which we are united than on which we differ, and that the former is more.important? We are not competitors for the orthodox or heterodox suffrages and support of the people, but we are humbly trying in all ways we can to build up a Christian community. There are a score of ways in which we might help one another, if we could only come together in the right spirit. And in many ways we already do help each other. I have Anglican friends who have given -me ideas I have found useful. I admit my inidebtedness to much which Canon Morley Stevenson has written. Conversations with other ministers yield their fruit, and we are all convinced that our power would be in- creased if we united together for fellowship, emphasising our unity instead of our disagree- ments. I am willing, and I think there are others also who are willing, to attempt to create a real Christian fellowship amongst ministers and laymen; and I make the sugges- tion because I think it is a good one, that we should all, Anglican clergymen, ministers of Congregational, Baptist, and Wesleyan de- nominations, Roman Catholic priests, and as many laymen and women as care to join, meet at stated times for (1) meditation and prayer, (2) discussion of the problems and difficulties which confront us all.

IRVINE LISTER.

Cairo-street Chapel, September 20th, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION AFTER THE WAR.

Sir,—Despite the absorbing interest of war, and despite the political truce, the problems of national education are appropriating a large share of public attention. They are penetrating also into the political arena; for the misfortune is that almost every step in educational progress becomes entangled with politics, an may be blocked or hampered in the House of Commons. The Committee of the National Education Association feel that they must prepare, even during war, for the coming struggle and they invite your readers to join them. The Associa- tion has taken a leading but unassuming part on behalf of popular education in most of the struggles since 1870; but the great result is yet to come. Our programme is very simple; it is the full and free development of the intellectual capacity of all, under the guidance and main- tenance of public authorities controlled by pub- lic opinion. In -pressing towards this mark we are tied to no party, no creed, no sectional in- terest; almost alone amongst organisations we are free to pursue all developments of national education in public interest alonm

Whilst the -war lasts we are not proposing to ask for a campaign fund. Our immediate pur- pose is to enrol men and women who care about national education, so that we can send them our papers, receive their suggestions, :invite them to our meetings, and in other ways ,pre- pare to have their co-operation at hand when the time for prompt action arises. The nominal enrolment fee (55.) will cover the cost entailed by the effort to secure this object. Preparation and organisation is our aim so that after the war, when molly other problems will force themselves upon statesmen and the public, we as a united body may be ready to keep education problems to the front. JOHN MASSIE, Chairman. 'A. J. MUNDELLA, Secretary. National Education Association, Canton House, Westminster, S.W.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 23, 1916.

SOLDIERS AND Itlf CHURCH.

Rev. H. Ogden Blames Outside

Influence For Failure.

Last • Sunday evening at Golborne-street Baptist Church, the Pastor, Rev. H. Ogden, preached from the topic, "Has the Church failed ?" taking for his text Ephesians v., 23, "Christ is the Head of the Church." The sub- ject had been suggested by the utterance of an officer invalided home from the Front. Ex- pressing his opinion about chaplains at the Front, he said, "New padres who joined the mess were treated with indifference. When a padre received ' honour in the mess he had earned it. Right down in the minds of the men who had been in this hell was the query, "What had the Church been doing these many centuries?"

The padres, as representatives of official Christianity, were under that cloud. What had the Church been doing these many cen- turies? "It is," said Mr. Ogden, "obvious that to prevent this European War the Church as an international organisation has done little. She failed to restrain the passion of kings and governors, and when war broke out she went down like a reed. The reason is not far to seek. There have been other heads than Christ. Allegiance to her risen• Lord has been con- ditional, and the conditional has destroyed her power. Lloyd George said concerning the power of the Church 'There is no evil could with- stand her combined forces if she were united.' Is she were united! What a significant if! The Church in the presence of gigantic evils has been at the best semi-helpless. The reason is not far to seek. A State Church financed by the State has her master to consider. Finance is a power- ful influence even in Churches. How otherwise could the Lutheran Church have been quiescent whilst Bernhardi preached that right is right so long as it is convenient? A State Church too that was Protestant.

Helpless In England As Well As In Germany. "But why be content with a German ex- ample? No one but must reverence the piety of Anglicans, and yet the powerful Church of England has been semi-helpless in the face of immense social evils. A Free Church is not without its bane. Free Church ministers' lips have been silenced, or their speech rendered innocuous because the leading deacon or society 'steward resents such plain speech. The trouble with the Church was and is that she has other masters than her Lord Jesus Christ., In the face of a united International Church what Emperor or his son would have dared to break the European peace? They dared because kings and courts were the masters of the Church. The Church should enthrone the, Christ and bow to no other master and her power would return. "The Church has had her triumphs. She has striven to bring up the young in the fear of the Lord. She has given of wealth of money and lives to carry the Gospel to heathen lands. In- dividuals and isolated communities have fought the battle of the oppressed.. The failure of the Church has been relative rather than complete. She may be bankrupt, but she has some assets, but when Christ is the head of all the Church then will be ushered in the glorious day."

FRENCH TOWNS OF INTEREST.

My Dear Children,— I have had a. very entertaining set of letters from you this week respecting the towns in France you would like to visit. Some of the towns named are not in France. Mons and Ypres are in Belgium, and however good an essay is, if it does not answer the question put, it cannot succeed. You must all remem- ber that at school, for many marks are lost through answering questions in the wrong way. I remember when I was a boy dividing a sum that should have been subtracted. I did the division correctly, but as a subtraction sum it was no use at all. It lost me a few marks and a big prize, so I have always been careful since to answer exactly the question I am asked. .

I have given the prize th 4 week to 813\ Niece Emily Marshall,

183, Winwick-road, who attends Silver-street School, an old member of the Corner, and a regular contributor to itS columns. This week we will have for our competition an essay on

Comforts That Are Needed For Our Soldiers At

The Frcnt.

Winter is now eoming along, and comforts will be badly needed almost at /once. As you will see in to day's "Examiner," an appeal is being made for money with whieh to buy material to be made into socks, mits and other useful articles for the soldiers. That subject Should, I think, bring a lot of answers, for you are all interested in doing something for,our soldiers. Your loving, UNCLE JACK.

women are about to patent a number of devites which are improvements on these. Thousands of women, new to industrial employment, work sewing machines and make khaki caps, over- alls, &c. There are no slagkers among women, who are eager and quick to learn, and punc- tual and regular in their attendance_ at the works. Satin for Tailor-Mades.

Apparently taffetas han at last reached the zenith of its popularity, and this autumn, at any rate, it will give place to satin. The latter fabric combines excellently with cloth, and tailors are using the two for the new one- piece dresses and coats. Velvet and satin is another favourite combination for the autumn, and each and all look well with fur. Dark colours are to prevail, various shades of wine being specially favoured for the moment. Rather freakish and very ugly were the skirts of uneven length that had a slight following a little time ago, on account of their novelty; they have, however, vanished, and fashion now decrees that the shortness must be at the back. The slight lifting is rather smart, for it is the right Place. Skirts are a trifle narrower and decidedly longer; exaggeration in any form is happily considered bad taste at the present time. The Piquant Small Muff.

Fashion has always been given to extremes, and this winter the fashionable muff is very small, scarcely big enough to accommodate the hands of the wearer. The big muff had advan- tages; it was a most useful receptacle for small parcels, and as it covered a considerable por- tion of the body it was conducive to warmth. There is something piquant, however, in the small melon-shaped muff that is first favourite at present, and it goes 'very well with the fur- trimmed - tailor-made. Big muffs will not dis- appear this winter, more especially when made in strands of expensive peltry. The new collars, capes and stoles are almost grotesque in shape, and chiefly built of stranded furs that cover the "ears and chin. They can be worn in different ways, open or closed, and are lined with magnificent brocades, often veiled with white or coloured chiffon. The fox skins that have been donned all the summer will be modish throughout the coming season, the head and paws of the animal being used, to finish the ends and back. Benzoate of Soda. -

Communications have reached the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries referring to a state- ment Which has appeared in the public Press to the effect that benzoate of soda may be used to replace sugar in the preparation of jam. The Board are advised that benzoate of soda is quite unsuitable for the purpose in question, and desire to warn the public against its use in jam-making. Serious results might follow an attempt to substitute this material for sugar.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARKIRIE.

Royal and Noble Artists. British royal and aristocratic ladies do not, in these democratic days, despise authorship, whilst not a few are first-rate sculptors and painters. That versatile Princess, the widowed Duchess of Argyll, is said to have contributed —under an assumed name—a number of learned articles to leading reviews and papers. She, too, is a noted sculptor. The Queen of Rou- mania, another British Princess, has published several books and has a natural gift for lan- guages and an innate taste for art. The Dowager Duchess of Buckingham and Chandos, who exhibited her pictures in 1912, is now arranging another display on behalf of war charities. Her Grace has made a reputation as a painter of portraits and landscapes, and is also an author and a great traveller.

Lady Dorothy Fielding. When it was announced that King George had decided to award the Military Medal to women there was much speculation as to who -would be the first recipient of that coveted distinction. The first member of the gentler sex to be so decorated was a French girl—. Mdlle. Emmellienne Moreau, the heroine of Loos, as she is called.. The second recipient is a .Welsh woman, Lady Dorothy Fielding, the daughter of the Earl of Denbigh. Her work for the nation began in September, 1914, in Belgium, where, with her heroic friends, Mrs. Knock and Miss Chisholm, she tended the wounded for month's in the cellar of a house close to the firing-line. In addition to other work, Lady Dorothy distinguished herself as an ambulance driver at Ypres. She has re- ceived the highest Belgian decoration from King Albert, and the . Croft de Guerre from the French Government. Tributes to Women Workers.

Lady Scott, the widow of the'great explorer, has been working for a long time in a muni- tion factory. She was most desirous to procure a weekly half-holiday for the women under her, and exacted a promise, from the manager to grant the favour, provided a certain maxi- mum was attained during the week. The girls' output .exceeded the highest estimate, and has been maintained ever since. Women have mastered the handling of eighteen- pounder shells, and now finish . and pack them. They also work wonders at lathes, presses, drills, and machines requiring great care and exactitude. Men claim nearly all the inventions used for the various processes, but

FARM AND GARDEN.

CROPS UNDER FRUIT TREES.

Fruit growers on a large scale seldom trouble with under crops, chiefly because they inter- fere with horse cultivation between the trees, and the crops are not considered worth the extra expense of hand cultivation. Smaller growers, however, have to make the most of their limited ground, and with them under- cropping is common. In the Evesham district, for instance, where most of - the holdings are small, and hand cultivation is tha general rut?, many crops are grown under the fruit trees. Strawberries are a very suitable crop to grow among young fruit trees, and if only a single row is planted between every two rows of trees there is no injury to the roots. If strong runners can be obtained for planting at once there will be a little fine fruit next summer, and in the second year the plants will be In their prime. After the third season the trees will probably have grown enough to shade the strawberries, which must then come out. This is a crop to grow only between young trees, but it is possible to grow winter and spring .crops under much older trees, becau,se at these seasons there are no leaves to shade the ground, while the bare branches afford welcome shelter.

Other suitable crops to grow under fruit trees are cabbages, lettuces, and wallflowers, all of which can be planted out in September. Spring cabbages and lettuces do very well under the shelter of the leafless trees, and the crops are off before the leaves come again. They can be in single rows, cabbages 15in. and lettuces Bin. apart in the rows. Wallflowers are largely grown under the plum trees in the Evesham district; the plants can be 12in. to 15in. apart in the rows.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160923_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
PACIFYING THE BULL.

In a cornbelt neighbourhood three bulls have been simply and cheaply pacified. One was a fence-breaker and the other two were belliger- ent. Now all three behave themselves and remain peacefully in their own pastures. They have been chained. Attached to the nose ring, each of these bulls wears a 3ft. length of chain about as heavy as the old-fashioned trace chain. So long as the bull is quiet and deliberate in his movement the chain seems to cause no pain, but a quick' throw of the head or a poke through the fence gives his nose a sharp jerk by the chain. The automatic and instantaneous punishment for such actions has taught them how to behave. Each of these bulls is allowed to run with the herd all the time. The last one to be treated had become so mean that it was not safe for a man to go near him. Finally he was driven into the barn and tied by the head for weeks. Then it was learned that the other two bulls were successfully, conquered. A breast chain from the team harness was then snapped into his ring, and he was turned loose. He had a stubborn fight for two weeks with the chain. At first he would bellow and paw the dirt when anyone came near, but when his foot would catch the chain he squirmed with pain and quieted down. Even then he would stand his ground and refuse to be driven away until his chain was struck smartly with a club. The old fellow bent his nose ring and injured his nose —and evidently did some thinking. Now he takes gentle care of that chain and allows people to take care of themselveg. This is a simple method. Its one of the cases where the easy and inexpensive process is better than some elaborate device. It takes time, patience, coigne, and eternal vigilance to manage unruly 'bulls. The chain is always fearlessly on the job, and the bull knows it. It is worth using on bulls inclined to take too many privileges with strangers or. their keepers.—" Breeders' Gazette."

===== 0002601_19160923_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

BORDER CARNATIONS AND PICOTEES.

These flowers are among the most beautiful grown to-day, and have been cultivated in Eng- land for centuries. It appears they were intro- duced by the Normanf and grown in monastic gardens for their beauty and fragrance. Dur- ing the last few years rapid strides have been made in the cultivation of carnations by florists, and now there are some really grand varieties in commerce. What is being aimed at is stiff- necked stems, erect blooms, non-bursting calyces and good growers. The improvement made in this direction is remarkable, and also in the size and beauty of the blooms. Border carnations give a wide range of colours, which add to their charm. As regards culture; any good ordinary soil will do, deeply dug and plenty of good rotted manure mixed with it., Raise the beds about 6 to 9in. for drainage, and plant about 12 to 15in. apart and make firm. Carnations should be grown in a separate besl to get best results. They also like soot, lime ' and salt during the growing season. Stake up as the flower spikes appear and disbud if size of bloom is desired.

October is thee best month to plant, and catalogues may be. had' from the carnation specialists.

Cuttings of all kinds may be taken now; insert in sandy soil and keep near the glass. Chrysanthemums may be disbudded if size of bloom is desired, and plants will be well bene- fited by an application of liquid manure. COYNE.—In loving memory of our dear son, No. Wm. Coyne, aged 24 years, No. 2350, 4th South Lancashire Regiment, who fell in action 25th Sep- tember, 1915. Fondly we loved him, he is dear to us still. But in grief we must bend to God's Holy Will, Our sorrow is great, our loss hard to bear, , But angels, dear son, will guard you with care. Deeply mourned by his MOTHER & FATHER. COYNE.—Private Wm. THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 23, 1916

1 THE PLIGHT OF GERMANY.

1_

r, ,

f ,1#

About twelve months ago, when the Rus- sians were applying a check to the advance of the Germans, after what had admittedly been a terribly trying time for all the Allies, it was suddenly giv,en out that Germany had shotfher bolt in the East. Subsequent events have proved that interpretation of the situation to have been a true one, though Germany thought Russia had been put out of the fight. We think we may no* say finally that Ger- many • has shot her bolt, in the West, as well as in South-East Europe. A few Weeks ago there was a suggestion thathe Germans were about •to shorten their lines; it is now main- tained that they are se firmly held that they eCuld,not retreat if they desired to do so. The prospect before them is surrender or annihila- tion, in our part of the arena at all events. It is estimated that between July 1 and Sep- tember 18 we captured no fewer than 500,000 prisoners on the three main front's, and the killed and °wounded must ,have made up a horrible record. Since that date we have

killed Boches, at an increasing rate, and' we find, as we might expect to find, a chastened tone in the enemy Press. The" Strassburger Post," for instance, -mournfully remarks: "We must not expect the 'battle of the Somme io cease, until the Allies are short of munitions, and that contingency has not yet arisen. The kar is no longer a matter for joy as formerly." The Viennese " Neue Freie Presse " eas discovered that "This is no longer war; it is extermination. It would 'appear that the offensive now so fiercely in progress on the Somme has for its aim simply the necessity of killing. No tactical idea is apparent; it, is simply butchery. One might suppose that the Anglo-French'Governments have made lin their Minds to kill a fixed num- ber of Germans per week or per month." It is a blunt way of stating the case, but it is an accurate way. Nothing but the slaying or the capture of men on the spot where they stand, where they have sinned against all the laws of God and man, will suffices and it is a happy augury that they are beginning to recognise the fact that we have now taken the " butchery " business, of which they once nearly had a monopoly, out of their hands. Our airmen have beaten the Germans to a stand, our inventors have given us modern chariots, our workpeople have given us the ships, the guns, and the shells—and, as we said, the German has shot his bolt. There is evidence that Germany is now short of muni- tions—that se cannot keep up with the pace we have set, a res;ult which redounds to the credit of the worker at home as much as the coming` shortage of German men redounds to the credit of our valiant representatives in the trenches. In the light of the last three months' experience we can well believe that the war has, not been " a matter of joy," as it was " formerly," beyond the Rhine.

AN ARCH OF CRUTCHES. Unique Feature At A Popular Wedding. An unusual incident at weddings, perhaps unrecorded up to the present, was that which attended the marriage of Mr. Henry Collin, of Winmarleigh-street, and Miss Hammond, matron of the Infirmary, which took place on Saturday at St. Paul's Church. As many as thirty wounded soldiers collected at the approach to the church as early as nine o'clock, and when the happy pair left the build- ing after' the ceremony they had to pass, laughingly and. triumphantly, beneath an arch of crutches. , The nursing staff of the Infirmary hail pre- viously gathered in the hall and corridor to wish their popular matron good lick and God- speed. The officiating clergy were the ,Rev. G. %Fab ' given, vicar r 4 Orford, assisted by the Rev. d. Downham,`curate of St: Paul's. The bride 'was attired in a tailor-made blue coat and skirt and a picture hat, and wore as ornaments a diamond and gold brooch, which - had been, presented to her by the Ladies' Com. mittee of the Infirmary, .and a gold watch bracelet presented to her as a compliment to Mr. Henry Collin, who has for many years acted as president of the Master Builders' Association. The bride was given away by Mr. J. H. Smethurst, and Mr. C. E. Bunch, of Bridge House, acted as best man. Mr. and Mrs. Collin are spending the honey- moon in Blackpool. It is interekting to record that in addition to the contracting parties, those signing the register were Mr. and Mrs. ,Smethurst, Mr. Bunch, and the Misses Marion and Ada. Collin (daughters of the bridegroom), the last two having also tastefully decorated the chancel \with beautiful flowers. -- The public presentations which the bride Ins received have alre9dy been recorded, and we have now to add„with great pleasUre that Mr. Collin has been the recipient of a canteen of, cutlery (supplied by Messrs. Eustance and Co.), from the Conservative Club, of which he is hon. secretary. Mrs. Collin having resigned her *position at the Infirmary, she has been succeeded by Miss Strachan, who has held a similar poht at the .'',2.-Vallasey Hospital, Liscard.

ROLL OF HONOUR. uPOTTER.—In loving memory of my dear nephew, Private Arthur Potter. who died of disease in Mesopotamia on July 21st, aged 21 years. God knows ho W much we miss him, He knows the tears we shed. And whispers, "Hush, he only sleeps, Your loved one is not dead." The happy hours we once enjoyed. How sweet their memory still: No morning dawns, no night returns, But what we think of him. AUNT EMMIE, COUSINS JACK ec JIM. Priestley-street.

GOSSIP.

I hear that the Mayor-elect is to be fixed upon at the General Purposes Committee on Monday. There is only one possible Mayor- elect at the moment, and that is the present occupant of the chair. I should say Alderman Peter Peacock will preside over our municipal destinies next year, and thus set up a record of a, four-years-continuously-in-office Mayor.

Under the headline, "The Might of the Mite," the "War Savings Journal" for September; published in London, begins, "This apposite title for an article on the small investor and the war is taken from the report of a War Savings meeting in the 'Warrington Examiner?"

Alone I did it. I was always good at arresting headlines—as the maid servant said when she took her mistress's new hat for the evening.

Lieutenant-Colonel George Crosfield, p. 5.0., is again at The /rowers, Bewsey, and knocking about the town generally in a taxi.

He attended the funeral of Dr. Adams at Lymm otr Tuesday afternoon, and, after liter- ally jumping out of his car, joined his broth.r officers. Colonel Crofffield was in mufti, and his artificial foot was encased ;in a brown boot.

Mr. Frank Kay, a colleague of mine on "The Examiner" staff, rho enlisted soon after war was declared and .has been in Egypt and Gallipoli almost continuously since, is one of those who have had no leaves of absence.

• He writes to me this week, saying he hopes in quarters. He now hopes he " may have a spell at home next Christmas—if not at Easter; if not, then August; if not, at Christmas. Any- how, he lives in hope and satisfaction, and is, in fact, a good English soldier who is happy to take thing as be finds them.

"I am not alone," he says, "for I know lots of fellows who, although they grumble and grouse about soldiering in Egypt, never lose hope. They all look forward with keen antici- pation to going to 'Blighty,' but, be the delay ever so long, they are always cheerfully optimistic and never downhearted." •

An object lesson-surely to some people who "grouse" at home at the least inconvenience to which they may be put.

The Corporation are,getting on very well with the repaving of Sankey-strwet. and soon light- footed wayfarers an heavy-rolling vehicles will be thoroughly appreciative of the roadway.

• In the meantime the romantic young Men and maidens parading at night delight in the fairy- land staging which the 'red, hanging lamps suggest.

It is not often that the Corporation creates a diversion—the Bewsey 'bus has to get 1.0 Market Gate via Rylands-street.

For the nonce in many months there has been no sitting of either th 9 Warrington, Warrington Rural District, or Runcorn Rural District Tiibunals this week.

I wonder where the members have been enjoying di" vacation. Considering the " cer- tain liveliness" which has arisen, probably some unsuccessful 'appellants who have been before the Tribunals would like them to spend it in a little black dug-out in the Et, but I May say that several, have -been to a " Little Grey Horne in' the Wet"—that .at the Royal Court Theatre.

Nearly a month ago the members of the Gas Committee received with sympathy tile sugges- tion that, owing to the darir.7l._.,,s of the streets at night, lampposts shpuld lie painted white in the middle so as tfi indicate their proximity to pedestrians sensitive to paia when colliding with a harder substance than ?iesh.

The white armlets began to appear on the lamp-posts this week. vs. ,

I have been privileged this week to see a letter written by Private Donovan, of the Irish Guards, three days before he was wounded. He was right in the thick of the fighting, as became an Irish Guardsman; but he shows the Army is being well-fed. •

"Do not send me any more bacon," he wiites home, "as we are ,getting more of it than we can eat. Cake is the most welcome out here, because it is so scarce." I fancy the trenches are not the only places where cake is scarce just now.

"I got the P.O. you sea. It came in handy, because we were in a well-populated village, and I got five francs for it, and five francs is a nice little sum to have a scout round with in a busy little French town." With bacon plenti- ful and French towns near the fighting line that are worthy of a " svut round," things would appear to be not so bad in Northern France.-

" MR. B. T. PARKES.

Appreciation Of Temperance Worker

Who Goes To The Colours.

Mr. B. T. Parkes Ole well-known Temperance and social worker, has joined up, and the fact has been made the occasion for wishing him .the best of luck and of placing on record an appreciation of the valuable services lie has rendered in many directions.

Warrirgton and District Citinns Temperance

League.

The following resolution, moved by the Rev. T. T. James and seconded by Mr. J. Griffiths, has been passed by the above League:— . “That-this meeting of the Citizens' Temper- ance League desires to place on record its high appreciation of the work done by Mr. B. T. Parkes as secretary, its sense of the value of his efforts on behalf of the Temperance cause, and to express to him it good wishes while on service with the Colours, and to hope that he may be speedily at liberty to resume his place in this organisation." \ Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church.

At the quarterly meeting recently held, the following resolution was passe& proposed by Mr. J. Griffiths and seconded by Mr. A. E. Hoyle:—

"At this quarterly 'meeting we were reminded of the exacting toll the war is still making on the nation's manhood. The following officials have already. responded to the call :—Brothers Bailey, Metcalfe and Floyd, and now our esteemed and respected Brother Parkes (cir- cuit secretary) has received the call. As this, in all probability, will be the last opportunity of meeting Brother ,Parkes officially, we think it fitting to place .on record our ,high apprecia- tion'-of the invaluable services he has rendered to the/Circuit for so many years. As super, intendent of the Sunday School, local preacher, and circuit Secretary he has shown qualities of able administration and good service. We are gad to Incl.,- that his high character is known Outside the Church. A few years ago the,Citi- zens' Temperance League appointed hi'm their secretary. In this 'position he is best known to the public. His work for Temperance has won him the respect bf alarge circle of friends. We deplore the fact that the exigencies of this tragic war are tearing from our side men like Brother- Parkes,: We pray that the Almight3' Father may watch over him in the interval of separation and may it please Him in His bwn good tithe to restore him to our midst."
