THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMIER 23, 1916.

A HOLIDAY IN IRELAND.

Some Notes On War-Time Lights And Shadows.

LIGHT RAILWAYS NEEDED.

By C. E. PARKER.

It might be thought that in choosing 'the shores of Sheepshaven in the north of Ireland as the scene of ouf family holiday we might get out of the shadow of the war. We soon realised that this; could not be even in this remote part of stony Donegal. It is true Ire- land has no conscription, and no doubt there are many who ought to be at the Front who ,remain at home. But there are others, Protest- ants and Catholics, Nationalists and anti-Rome Rulers, who have flocked to the Colours. It seemed impossible to find a single hamlet that had not sent its representatives to the great war, and in Many a humble Donegal cottage, with its earthen floor_ and dent thatched roof, there were anxious .mothers eager to get the opinion of an Englishman as to when the war would end, while not a few were mourning for their sons, and would not be comforted because they were not. The shadow of the war was apparent in many,of the industries. The little herring, fleets were depleted; some of the fishers had gone' as soldiers, others as mine sweepers, while the GOveinment-provided' boats were called away on more important work. Generally speaking, the war has brought pros- ;)erity to the agriculturists in Ireland, and the localities within reach of Tuarkets or railway connections are doing well. In most of Donegal the little fanners are so far from a market that they have not shared proportionately in the general prosperity. These are to a large extent, self-dependent, growing what they require for their own use, and only occasionally ha,ing ‘ur- plus stock to market. Still even these are ex- periencing the stimulus occasioned by the great ~y3rld struggle.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
flax G,-owing.

Many farmers in Donegal have, for the first time, grown flax, and this has proved a profit- able venture. The amount of flax grown in Ireland this year is probably seven .or eight times the normal quantity. &fore the wal-eire- land grew about one-fifth of the flax Manufac- tured in the country, now nearly half is grown. The value since the war began has about trebled. and I believe has reahied .£l2O a ton. This is after, if has been 'cleaned .and scutched. We can still .get flax from Russia, but the Bel- gian .supply, about a- third of the whole, is en- tirely 'cut off. heard of one farmer who affirmed that ,he had made £7O clear from an acre of flax, but this is very exceptional.- The flax plant has a thin wiry stem branched to- wards the ton to early the pretty sky-blue flower which is the blossom of the bolled fruit which contains the linseed. The stalk grows about three feet in, height, and is somewhat stronger than wheat. The fibres run in strands between the skin and the woody pith forming the hollow tem. 'Flax, when ripe, is not cut, but is pulled up by the roots, bound in sheaves and nut in some adjacent water to 'steep. The object of this steeping is to dissolve by fermentation the glutinous matter adhering to the fibres.' At the end of a fortnight it is taken out and dried in the fields. It is then bruised further to dis- entangle the fibre, then sent to the flax mills to he scutched and spun. The damp climate of Treland is specially adapted both to the growth and the spinning of flax, but the plant takes -nuch out of the ground, and cannot be grown on the same land in successive years. The rota- tion is potatoes, corn, flax. The Harvest of the Shore.

The making of kelp from seaveed is an indus- try followed all along the northern coast of Ireland. The seaweed is cut and gathered at low tide and brought up to dry by carts or by the help bf donkeys with panniers suspended over the back. It is then spread on the grass or

-rol to dry, and when dry burnt to -an ash. This ash is bagged and conveyed to the nearest means of transit to Glasgow or London, where_ iodine and .other products are extracted. It is also ak valuable manure, and is now in. demand for the hungry flax crops. The price the farmers of Donegal were getting before the war for kelp ash was .23 10s. per ton on rail; it is now at least .26 ss. All the small farmers along the sea coast turn their .attention to kelp when not busy with their land crops. Seaweed is also largely used direct as manure for crops.

Peat Ireland's Fuel.

, The getting in during the summer of the winter's supply of fuel—that is, turf or peat— engages a good deal of the energy and time of the Irish cottagerS. There are many parts of the country in which no coal is ever used.. Peat is. the only fuel, and often has to be hauled several miles. I have seen donkeys with their panniers full of peat on the coast of Donegal from the base of the hills, at an elevation of 1,500 feet, coming down. the bridle paths to the cottages near where I stood. I do not suppose that the weight of peat. carried by each donkey could be more than 701 b., this would be equal at best to 351 b. of coal, and it would probably take two or three hours to do a single journey. To this time and lribonr must be added the cut- ting .and drying of the peat earlier in th eason. The depth of the peat beds varies considerably; in Donegal it is generally not more than two or three feet, and when this has been cut there is often, nothing but rock below. In. such cases the pasturage is spoilt, and this is a serious pro- blem. Not only is the peat exhausted and ceases to grow, but the grazing area is diminished. Xhother result of cutting out the'peat, where the land is low lying, is that it is often, cut below the drainage level, in consequence the ground becomes waterlogged and rendered' unfit for crops. Thousands of acres are, being rendered' useless in Ireland fro nl this cause, and such low. eating ought to be made illegal. Generally, the right of cutting :tail on the .11.111 s goes with the holding; or a small charge is made/but in such cases turf can only be cut for the use of the teuant, and must not be Marketed. •

Other Uses For Peat.

Many efforts have been made to utilise peat for *her purposes than 'household -fires. Much motley has been spent and much lost in Ireland in experiments. While there have been many failures, some success has been achieved. Air- dried peat yields a capital power gas, and fac- tories are being run entirely With it; much to the owners' advantage while coal is so dear. No, doubt. much more could be done in this direction, but the factory must not be top far from the supply, as the bulky character Of the peat makes transit expensive. A further de- velopment of light railways would bring Much more of this fuel into use. Peat is now largely used for litter for horses and ourimports for this purpose from the. horses, and Ger- many before the war amounted to about 100,000 tons annually, a good deal of this went to Ire- land. This has now stopped to Ireland's bene- fit. A considerable quantity of peat dust is used in the making of nuilassine meal for cattle food. also for packing eggs and fruit. Sphagnum moss from peat. bogs is now extensively used in hospitals for dressing wounds, and volunteer parties are formed and go. out 'Pi Ith sacks to gather this moss. - The moss has then to be carefullY•picked over and all the hard substance taktcn out.. A use has lately been. found' for peat by rLi..ofessOr Bottomley. By inoculating peat with nitrogen-fixing bacteria he has produced a valuable fertiliser, and the Manchester Cot- poration has been granted the free use of his .proeess for a period. I have tried this 'bacterialged peat in my garden as a fertiliser for tomatoes; the result .has been an increased yield of 40 per cent. against a well-knoWn fer- tiliser. In this there is a great future for peat dust if the cost of manufacture can .be reduced. In Donegal the spinning-wheel and hand- loom are still in use for worsted and the fabrics made from it. I visited a cottage and found an old lady about eighty sitting by the peat fire busy spinning, and it was wonderful to see how' her aged .fingers manipulated the raw wool into an even thread by the help of her wheel: Three strands are then twisted together and become an even thread of three- ply, and from this the stockings are knit, and the famous Donegal tweeds, with their pungc.nt smell of peat reek, are Made:

Death of Miss Milner.

We record with regret the death of Miss Milner at her residence, Moore House, on Sunday night. An extensive landowner in Cheshire, she was well-known for her philan- thropy. Born in 1831, at Moore House, she resided there all her life, and so completed a 200 years' occupatiop of the house by her family. The 'funeral took place at Daresbury on Thursday morning.

COMFORTS NEEDED!

Prepare For Winter Campaign In France And The East.

VOLUNTARY WORKERS' URGENT APPEAL FOR £3OO.

It is now certain that our gallant troops will have to face the Adiscomforts of the trenches for another winter, and it is clearly the duty of those who will be enjoying the luxury of home life to see that those discomforts .are minimised as much as possible. Fortunately, there are many willing workers to make the much-needed woollen helmets, scarves and socks and similar articles; all they want to set them going to keep up the supplies is the money to buy the material. For this money the following appeal has been made:—

Comforts for our troops at the Front—France, Mesopotamia, Egypt and Salonica—and for our wouncled soldiers at home and abroad.

THE WINTER CAMPAIGN:

Dear Sir, or Madam, Since the outbreak of war, the women of Warrington have sent out many scores of thousands of pairs of mits, socks, shirts and other necessary and useful articles, largely for the men of our Territorial Battalions.

In December, 1915, the Governinent decided— in order to prevent overlapping, and waste—to place the direction of the distribution of com- forts in the • hands of Sir Edward Ward, as Director-General, and at whose request. a local branch of the National Committee was formed here. For this purpose the members of the ,Territorial Equipment Committee, the War- rington Women's Liberal Association, and the Cairo-street and Moore Working Parties and Miss White's Central Committee have amal- gamated and have dispatched from time. to time on the requisitions of the Director-General large quantities of comforts for the Army generally, but with the proviso that the claims and needs of our local battalions should have first place. Over 500 ladies have been helping in this great work for the past nine months, and are most anxious to continue doing so. At this moment many thousands of mits, socks, hospital shirts, slippers,. operation socks and other articles are urgently needed, and part of the work is in hand.

Our funds are almost exhausted ; we depend entirely upon voluntary help, and we cannot comply with the claims already made upon us without immediate and substantial financial support.

In all the fields of wkir our fellow townsmen are fighting, and when we:remember •the awful disipmfort and danger which has hitherto been inellitable in trench warfare in winter—frost- bite, rheumatism, chest troubles, trench fever -and; the like—there is not one of us who is not prepared to, do his or her best to mitigate to the utmost possible extent, the horrors and suffer- jugs which our heroic soldiers are facing so ' cheerfully. Winter, is rapidly approaching! Time -is short! The claims of our troops are urgent! Three hundred pounds is needed forthwith and more later! Please help!

Subscriptions may be sent to the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) president, to the Hon. Treasurer, Mr. Jno. Fairhurst, or to any of the undersigned. JAS. H. SMETHURST, Chairman, HENRY COLLIN, Hon. Sec., MISS WHITE, Holly House. Sankr-street.

THE LATE DR. ADATOS.

mpressive Funeral At Lytnm On Tuesday.

A fortnight to the hour since he stood with troubled mein and uncovered head and watched- the body of his beloved wife lowered into a grave in the peaceful slope of Lymna Churchyard, all that was mortal of •Dr. Adams himself was laid to rest therein. on Tuesday afternoon.

It was an impressive funeral. An autumnal day without the faintest ray of sunshine, the tendency to humidity in the atmosphere com- pleted the melancholy aspect, and it was with depressed. hearts that mourners and friends followed the bier into the church, where the Ilev. E. C. E. Carleton (vicar of Holy Trinity Church, Warrington) read the service before leading the procession to the graveside. The congregation was almost identical With that which assembled a fortnight before; his wife's friends were his friends. .There were many doctors, public and business gentlemen, and soldiers, and the general friends included scores of people from Warrington, particularly in. the neighbourhood of his residence, who attended to pay their tribute of esteem. Feelings of ,poignant grief were visible every- where.

"Weeping at the feet and head, I can see your falling tears, I' can hear your sighs and prayers."

Town Clerk Receives News Of His Son.

PRISONER IN PRUSSIA.

-Yesterday the Town Clerk received a tele- gram from Messrs. Cox and Co., Army bankers, to the effect that "Geneva Gent" telegraphs :

Captain 0. Lyon Whittle, Flying Corps, prisoner of war at Guterslohe, Germany.

Guterslohe (16 Ce) is a small town in West- phalia, about 39 miles south of Minden (16 Cd).

Capt. Whittle was transferred from the South Lancashire Regiment to the Royal Flying Corps, along with his companion, Capt. A. Skinner, on August 19. The two promptly got to work, for on August 31 they took part in a bombing raid in hostile territory. They failed to return, and the greatest anxiety' has been felt for them since. Yesterday's message will be received with universal pleasure, and the hope that similar good tidings may come to hand regard- .ing Capt. Skinner. Fp to lagt night nothing had been heard of ttat gentleman.

OFFICIAL CONFIRMATION.

Mr. J. Lyon Whittle received late last night a telegram from the War -Office notifying that Captain 0. Lyon Whittle, "previously reported missing, now officially reported prisoner .of war at Guterslohe."

FROM CAVALRY TO iNFANTRY,

Stationmaster's Son Promoted

To Second-Lieutenancy.

His many friends will be .pleased to hear that Mr. .Charles McDonald Armstrong, stepson of Mr. Gordon, station master at Bank Quay, and Mrs. Gordon,,, 46, Arpley-street, has been gazetted a , Second-Lieutenant in the King's Own yorkshire. Light Infantry. Second-Lieutenant Armstrong, who is twenty- .

SECOND-LIEUTENANT CHAS. M,.DONALD ARMSTRONG.

six years of age, has been in the Army for eight years. He enlisted in the 16th -Laric:_.rs, and transferred to the 17th Battalion, along with his step-brother, Edward A. Gordon, and they went to India together. They were drafted from India to France in November, 1914, and have served there since. His step-brother is at pre- sent sick in hospital. Second-Lieutenant Armstrong has only had three days' leave dur- ing his whole Army career. He was educated at Wycliffe School, and attended the Sunday school and church. Before enlisting he was employed in the offices of Messrs. Greenall's dis- tillery, to which place he went from the offices of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd.

Warrington Market _Prices.

At Wednesdays Warrington Market the price of new wheat ruled from Bs. 3d. to Bs. 6d. per 701 b., and 4s. to 4s. 3d. per 451 b. was realised for new oats. Flour realised 475. 6d. to 525. 6d. per 2401 b., thirds 2s. 4d. to 2s. 7d. per 201 b; bran, is. 7d. to is. 9d. per 201 b.; wheat straw, 70s. per ton.

Infretious Disease.

Last week the following new cases of infec- tious disease in Warrington were reported : Diphtheria, 2; erysipelas, 3; measles, 3; puer- peral fever, 1; tuberculosis, 6. It will be noted that there were no new cases of scarlet fever. There remained in the hospital the following local cases :—Scarlet fever, 'l.e; enteric fever, V; diphtheria, 7; phthisis (Sankey Hospital), 17; total, 37. Serious Street Accident.

A serious accident occurred in Winwick-road on Monday, when Mrs. -Martha Hankinson, 22, West-street, was knocked , down and rim over by a waggonette. Two privates of . the R,A.M.C.. from; the Lord Derby 'War Hospital took the injured woman to the Infirmary, where it was ascertained that both her legs were fractured. Mrs. .Hankinson, who is 58 years of age, is further prostrated from shock and lies in a very critical state.

New School Mahallers. The Rev. B. Schofield has been appointed foundation manager and correspondent of the Ladies' School of Industry. Miss Ferriman wrote to the Education Committee on Monday regretting that, as she had retired ' from her appointment at the Training College and was leaving Warrington, it became necessary for her to resign her position as a representative manager of the Smith-street School.—The Chairman (Councillor 11. Roberts) .said that Miss Ferriman's long experience in educational matters had made her an exceptionally able and valuable member, and he moved that the committee place on record their appreciation •of her services.—This was • seconded by Alder- man Smethurst and carried.

Motor Cars Collide.

When Private Thomas Pettit, Mechanical Transport, Aril3y Service Corps, was driving a motoi car belonging to the Ministry of Muni- tions ..ip Bold-street and in the direction of Sankey-street on Monday afternoon, he collided with another motor car driven and owned by Mr. Henry Charles Sutton, 14, Upingham-road West Derby, Liverpool. Mr. Sutton's cir was cominit along Palmyra-square to Suez-street, and the impact of the two vehicles was very violent. The civilian's Car .was knocked over on the footpath at Mr. Forshaw's office, and Mr. Sutton was slightly bruised about the chest. Pettit was uninjured. Both cars were damaged in the collision, and were 'subsequently removed to the Lion Hotel Garage.

Parish Church Musical Recital.

A recital of sacred music in aid of the Lord Kitchener Memorial Fund for disabled officers and men was given in Warrington Parish Church on Sunday afternoon. The attendance was a fair one. The choir sang the chorus "Be not afraid," from Mendelssohn's "Elijah." Beethoven's Hallelujah Chorus, and the anthem " 0 gladsome light." The follow- ing sang solos: Master Neville Davies, "Hear ye Israel," Mendelssohn ; Mr. J. B. Poole, "And God said" and "Now heaven in fullest glory," Haydn; Mr. J.. Sephton, "Be thou faithful," Mendelssohn ; and Mr. Crowe, "He was despised," Handel. "Overture," Handel; Cantilene," Salome; and "0 Sanctissima," Lux, were organ solos\ played by Mr. F. M. Darby, the organist.

A Patriotic Movement. In consideration of the prevailing depletion of clerical staffs, the centrally situated Copying and Typewriting Bureau at Wood's College, 15, Bridge-street, has placed its resources and staff of expert typists at the service of patriotic firms in this district free of charge "for the duration of war." When .the "rush comes" and extra clerical help is needed, you• should 'call with your requirements at Wood's College. Provid- ing the stationery is supplied, the Bureau will undertake your envelope addressing, circulars, form letters, postcards, specifications, duplicat- ing, MSS., &c. Strict privacy and speedy despatch. guaranteed. Anyone' engaged in any branch of philanthropic and national work is especially invited to place 'work to be typed there. -

===== 0002601_19160923_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
Thank God 1

Sir Gilbert Greenall, accompanied by Masters Gilbert and Edward Greenall, attended a matinee last Saturday given in the Chester Picturedrome in aid of the Allied Farmers' Relief Fund. In thanking the management of the Picturedrome, Sir Gilbert explained that the Royal-Agricultural Society collected £70,000 for helping the farmers who had suffered during the Franco-German war. . That sum enabled them to 'do the Fretfch farmers a Food deal of good; but the present needs with Servia, Bel- gium, Poland and Northern France devastated would be very much greater. Some said that an appeal made now was premature, but they must be ready with the funds at any moment if they were to do any good. As a matter of fact a great deal had already been done in the parts of France from which the enemy had been cleared; already ' a good amount of breeding stock had been sent and a lot of seeds „and agri- cultural implements, but after the war there would be an enormous amount of that work to be done, and they must have available fUnds„ "Thank God," concluded Sir Gilbert, "the war has not taken place in our land, and that our farms and homesteads and buildings have not all been devastated .as those poor people's were." The proceeds amounted to over £5O.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of Mr. John Alban Tivnin.

The death occurred on Saturday', September 9th, of Mr. John Alban Tivnin, who was serving at .Cardiff as a! private in the Royal Defence Corps. Mr. Tivmn,--who 'resided at 54, Cock- hedge-lane, has had a life-long. connection, as also had his father, with the Vir.hitecross- Wire Works. He entered the employ of the company at the age of eleven years and served them as a wire cleaner until he joined the Army fifteen months ago. His death, which came suddenly was the result of hemorrhage of. the brain. The body was conveyed to 'Warrington, under the care of Sergeant Connor, who was also present at the funeral. The interment took place last Saturday at the Cemetery and a large number of friends and neighbours assembled in token !of respect. Bearers and buglers from the Bar- racks attended, as did also a firing party from Winwick. Father Willon performed the last rites and the members of the St. Mary's Young Men's Society met the Cortege at the Cemetery. The Mourners present were Mrs Tivnin (widow), Miss Sarah Tivnin and Miss Ellen Tivnin (daughters), Master John Tivnin and Master Jamee, Tivnin (sons), Mrs. Riley, Mrs. Murray and Mrs. Malam (sisters-in-law), Miss Lily Turner (niece) and Miss S. Lawless. Wreaths and floral tributes were sent by the officers of the Royal Defence Corps, the N.C.O.'s and men of the South Lancashire Regiment, Cardiff; "His Wife and family,' Mr. and Mrs. Riley and family, Mr. and Mrs. Warburton and family, "His sisters-in-law, Lily and Polly Tivnin."

Messrs. J. kounfield. and Sons carried out/the funeral arrangements.

The Huns.

'His wife, who reside % at 16, Brookland? street, off Padgate-lane; received on Wednesday a card from him which stated: "I am a prisoner of war, and stationed at Dulmen (Westf)." THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 23, 1916.

DIED FOR 1114EIR eOUNT*Y.

I very much regret to tell you that Lance- Corporal G. Maddock was killed in action on August 27th, whilst digging a trench with a working party. „ When I heard he 'had been killed it was a Treat shock to me, as he was one of the most relia:Ae and best men in the company. You. must console yourself in the fact that your husband has given his life for his King and;country." Lance-Corporal- Maddock; • who was thirty years of age, and the father of three children, was formerly employed by the WhitecrosstCo., Ltd. He served for five years in the Terri- torials prior to the war, and re-enlisted- two years agu next Christmas, and had been at the Front' sixteen. Months. As ,a boy he attended t. BarnabatiVehool. He was a good boWler, won several prizes when vfar..:ig for the Whitecro4s team. Mrs. Maddock has a brother and brother-in-law in the Army; the former is at present suffering from a trench disease and the latter has ''.leen wounded in the foot.

Additional Local Casualties in the Various War Areas.

LIEUTENANT WRIGHT. Killed. ,

Arthur Wright, son of Mr. and Ars. D. W. Wright,B Winwick-street. 0 _ _ _

A -letter from Captain E. D. Baskin was re- ceived on Tuesday morning. "It is with the deepest Sympathy," he said, "that I have to inform you that your son was killed this morn- ing. As I haVe just last my only brother, I can well understand what a heavy blow this will be, and I feel most deeoy for you in Your loss. We, too, shall !greatly, miss him. At two o'clock this morninc, he was on duty with some guns on an advanced point, when a large shell landed quite close to him and 'a big splinter went right •through his heart, killing him 'in- stantly." A letter has also been received from the deceased officer's servant, in which he expresses his great sorrow and pays Mr. Wright a splen- did tribute. .

Second-Lieutenant Wright secured his conhmi- sion in March, 1915, and took up duties with the Royal Warwickshire Regiment. After pass- ing a test for Machine gunnery he became attached to the Machine Gun Corps two months ago, and then he crossed to France. He was scholar of brilliant attainments. After being educated first at Arpley-street School and later at the Boteler \ Grammar School, he went to Manchester University, taking his B.A. and M.A., degrees. He then entered the teaching profession. He first took up duties as clatQical master at Hutton Grammar School, near Pres- ton, subsequently becoming second master at Eggar's Grammar School, Alton, Hants. lie belonged to St. Paul's Bible ,Class and when at home attended St. Paul's Church.

One Of Four Soldier-Brothers Who Was Only Eighteen.

Private Robert Smith, son of Mrs. Smith, 1, Bennett-street, and of the Machine Gun Corps, 'was killed on September 2nd of this year., lie

PRIVATE R. SMITH. Killed.

Fusiliers, are still serving; •

"Our Day" Approaches.

There is to he a local ;colebTation of --Our Day ", by the holding .of a Cafe Chantant in Parr Hall on October 19. "Our Day" will spread itself over the whole kingdom and the Dominions overseas; in fact, it Promises to be the most completely organised 'day" that the war has called forth. By the success of the world-wide effort it is hoped that the patronage—given for the lint time to an appeal of this kind—of the King,. who, as well as the Queen and Queen Alexandra, is a patron of the movement, will be thoroughly. justified. Our Day" last year brought £1,400,000 to the Red Cross. It is the "day" of everyone who Las the spirit and intuition tb feel for the men Who are cheerfully suffering, fighting, and falL log for 'those who remain behind;. of everyone, roan. or woman, who, from .whatever cause, is debarred from a direct share in the contest of the nations. For those reasons, and no less for a host of others, the organisers of "Our Day" holis that this year will surpass the record, not Inerely of last .year, but of all other days. It is for Britons, and the Britons beyond the seas, to ensure. that they are not disappointed. Already 33.600,000 flags linvp been ordered, and tnatlY are being sent to Malta, Gibraltar. Singa- Pore,;.St. Helena, and other outposts of Empire. Window cards, which are being. supplied, will, lit is hoped, be hung in the window of every "se WhPrp a soldier or sailor has gone to Serve the King, and thus help to keep alive the spirit of "Our Day."

DIED OF WOUNDS.

CORPORAL G. H. COX.

Ex-Organist Fatally Wounded

In The Chest.

Mrs. Cox, of 33, Wellfield-street, has had the , Mournful tidings of the death of her son, Cor- poral Geo. Hughes Cox, Royal Welsh Fusiliers, conveyed to her through one of his comrades. Writing from France under date 13th Septem- ber, Lance-Corporal J. C. Roff, -Machine Gun Section, said: "Just a line to let you know that your son George, who was wounded on the 11th, is reported since to have died of wounds; and I wish to express to you the heartfelt sympathy of the machihe gunners in your sad loss. He was a pal of mine since be 'joined the South. Lancs. He has been with me nearly all the time he was in France in my team, and his loss is deeply felt.- Let me know what I must do with his letters, •&c., as I am keeping them. He asked- me to write and let you know what had befallen him. He was a true and faithful soldier, and was .highly respected by all." A further letter was received by Mrs. Cox from a nursing sister in a base hospital, stating that her son had been received and died there. He had been struck in the chest.

Under date 13th September, Second-Lieutenant H. A. Parry, machine gun officer, wrote to Mrs. Cox: "It is my most painful duty to inform you that your son, George Cox; has died of wounds. His fellow machine gunners and my- self were exceedingly sorry to hear , the news this morning, and we deplore the loss of such a smart and popular man. A short time be- fore he was wounded early on Sunday morning I spoke to Mm while he was doing his turn as sentry, and stayed with him for a few minutes. He seemed very—km on looking out for Ger- mans. Later on when I came back that way 3 saw him wounded. He was wounded in the stomach by a rifle grenade. He bore the pain Very bravely' indeed. We put him on a stretcher and he was taken to the first-aid post as soon as possible, and from there to the hos- pital. We did not think that his wound was so serious because he was so brave over it. I can only hope that you will receive some con-

solation in the fact that he died through re- maining at his post under heavy rifle-grenade fire like a trim British soldier. I sympathise with you sincerely at having lost, such a fine son; he was a smart soldier and machine gunner, and very popular with everyone who knew him."

Corporal Cox enlisted first of all in the South Lancashire Regiment on January 22nd of the present year, but was subsequently transferred with a number of others to the Royal Welsh Fusiliers. He was drafted to France in June, and was attached to the machine gun section. This capable young 'soldier was in his 21st year. He was a well-known footballer, and played for' Warrington Excelsior F.C., and wa.- a good all-round athlete. He was the secretary of, the Buckley-street -Methodist Free Church Mission, and organist of the church.

MISSING.

CORPORAL H. B3SCOW.

'Former Ryls.ld3's Wiredrawer Missing.

Mr. and Mrs. Boscow, 22, Ashwood-avenue, would be glad of any news concerning their sort, Corporal Harold Boscow, .of the Rifle Brigade, who was reported missing aftei an engagement on Angust 25th. His parents last heard from him on the 24th August: Corporal Boscow, who is twenty-five years of age; enlisted in October, 1914; and 'went to the Front in the following November. He came home on leave in December, 1915, and after "e- -turning to the Front, was wounded •in Febru- ary. He again returned to the Front after a few months in England, and a few weeks ago entered the bombing section., He was educated at the National School, and was formerly employed as a wiredrawer by Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd. He wasln good bowler and billiard

player, and, by his prowess in these directions, won several prizes. •

Wo are pleased to state that Sergeant Brad- bury, of the R.W.F., son of ,Mr. W. Bradbury, 168, Orford-lane, who was admitted into a 'base hospital, on September 3rd r—rterlng from severe abdominal wounds, is now in an English hospital progressing satisfactorily.

WOUNDED.

SERGEANT J: JACKSON.

Has Had A Piece Of Scrap-Iron Taken From His Lee.

Mr. and Mrs. J. Jackson, 205, Knutsford- road, have been informed that their son, Sergeant John Jackson, King's Royal Rifles, has been wounded. Writing home he says he has had a piece of scrap iron taken from his leg, and is now going on quite nicely. He is at present in hospital at Colshqw Hall, Cheshire.

Sergeant Jackson, who is:. 24 years of age, enlisted in September, 1914, and has been in France eighteen months. He formerly worked for Messrs. T. LoCker and Sons, and was an old boy. of St. James' School, also being a chorister in St. James' Church Choir. His brother, Bombardier Percy Jackson, is at pre- sent stationed in Ireland.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL E. BOOTH.

Fine Soldier Who Did Not Know What Fear 'as.

Two letters have been the means of conveying to Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Booth, 22,. Rhodes- street, the .news of the wounding of their son, Lance-Corporal Edgar Booth, South Lancashire Regiment. The wounds are in the right shoulder, there being five altogether.

The first letter from a comrade was as follows: "I am sorry to let you know that your son, Edgar, got hit this morning, September 9th. He was one of my best 'friends, both out here and at home. You must not upset your- self, because I believe he is all right, and I dare say he will be removed to England. He was one of the old brigade, who came out here in 1915.".

The second letter, from Corporal L. Gough, ran: "It would not surprise me to hear of him being in England before this letter reaches you. We were great pals, as you know, and there is no doubt about it, he was one of the finest soldiers out here, and did not know what fear was. My one regret is that I was not on the spot when he was hit, !7o that I could give you more details. I can assure you he will be sorely missed 'both by me and his comradvs, as he is well known for the good work he did when a stretcher-hearer, as he always seemed to be ready for duty at all times."

Lance-Corporal Booth was brought to England and now lies progressing , well 4fl hospital at Southport, where he was visited by his parents on Monday. When wounded he was blown into a shell hole.

Lance-Corporal Booth, who is 20 years of age, enlisted on the outbreak of war, and went to France in February, 1R13.. He formerly worked for Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., is an old boy of Hamilton-street. School, and attended

Brick-street Mission. He was interested in football, and was a great admirer of music, he, himself playing a clarionette in the South Lancs. Band.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
Shrapnel Wounds Lead To Amputa- tion Of Leg.

We understand that Lance-Corporal T. Alcock, of the South Lancashire Regiment, who has had one, of his legs amputated owing to severe shrapnel wounds, is progressing as favourably as can be expected. News of the operation having been performed in a hospital in Kent was received last week at his home, 112, Chester-road, Lower Wa/ton. Lance-Cor- poral Alcock, who is unmarried and 28 years of any was educated at Arpley-street School, and before pining the Forces was employed by Messrs. R. A. -Naylor, Ltd., for a period of ten years. He enlisted in June of last year, and was sent out to the Front after only. three months with the Colours. His brother, Cor- poral J. B. Alcock, has had the distinction of being mentioned in dispatches.

RIFLEMAN MASON.

Praises Work Done For Wounded In A Wesleyan Hospital. The "Bath Herald" publishes some verses

written by Rifleman Steven Mason, a White- cross wiredrawer,- of the Rifle Brigade, and son of Mr. William Mason, 29, Napier-street, War- rington. It will be remembered that Rifleman Mason, who was wounded in severhl places, in- cluding his foot, had the pleasure of seeing the King and Queen when 'hair -Majesties visited' a. Weston-Super-Mare hospital in which ,he was then a patient. He has herd no fewer than nine operations with a view to saving his foot, but the dootors have not yet succeeded in •their task.. He is now a patient in the Kingswood V.A.'Hospital, Lansdown, Bath, and the verses -are in praise of the nurses and the treatment wounded soldiers receive in that institution.

• Kingswood School, of which the hospital is a part, is a school for Wesleyan ministers' Sons, and at the present time its tudents include two sons of the Rev. •W. T. • Derry, Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. The last verse has referene't to the fact that the Wesleyans have provided this accommodation;--- • Will the Wesleyans of, Great Britain , Accept Tommy's splendid thanks For the care' they give to Tommy And attention to his wants.? And When this war is over , • No matter where I roam, . I shall think of dear old Kingswood School, The place that gave me home.

PRIVATE R. ROWLEY.

Poisoned By Gas, Wounded, And Suffering From Shell Shock.

Private Harry Rowley; • South Lancs. • Regi- ment, and son of Mr. and Mrs. J. G. Rowley, 57, Nicholson-street, who was wounded a short time ago, is now in hospital suffering from shell shock. He is wounded in the left wrist. Writing to his parents he says be is able to walk about a little, and hopes to return to England soon. He suffered from the effects of gas poisoning last rear, but was soon able to take his place .% il 11 1-h' -n-nrades again. Private Roww,,o, vrht, nqd belonged to the South Lancashire Regi,flent for four years, volunteered at the beginning of hostilities for active service. He is 21 years of age, and after leaving Wycliffe School he was first employed by Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd.,Thut latterly was the employment of the L. and N.-W. Railway Co. He was also .a member pf the Church Lads' Brigade, belonging to St. Paul's: Company.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE F. P. DONOVAN.

Iped To Drive havarians Bach Over

Three Miles.

Private Francis .Patrick Donovan, son of Mr. and Mrs. John Done Van, 35, West-street,- who joined the Irish Guards . a few months before the War began, was wounded September 15th. He took part in the severe fighting ea-Oy in September, and in a letter written three days before- he was wounded he said a parcel froin home , had just reached him on coming out of the trenches-. '` or rather, where there should be trenches." He added, "It is a very rough quarter just hero, and our fellows are still going ahead and bringing in Prisoners every day. I am just beginning to think, we are winning. It is almost incredible how the British `Tommies' are playing the game in the thick of it; every man is really worthy of a V.C. That is the way we. are smashing German tyranny!' - The next letter was written in a Red Cross train in England, and announced that he had got two bullets through the right shoulder, but would soon be quite fit again. A third letter stated that he was in a hospital at Wrexham,

and was geting on famously. He added: "It was on the morning of September 15th that the Guards attacked the last stand of the Bavarians at about 6.30 a.m., nnd after we had pushed them back about three miles and a half I got two bullets through the shoulder." Private Donovan, who is a piano and violin player, is 41 years of age. Before joining the Army he was apprenticed to Mr. Dolan, builder, in the joiners' department. Ile attended St. Benedict's School and People's College. .

RIFLEMAN J. SMITH.

Schoolboys Raise £5O for War Funds. The scholars of the Parochiftl Boys' School, Church-street,, have subscribed the ;following sums of money since the beginning of the war : £l.O for the Belgian Refugees' Fund„ £5 for the Lord Derby Hospital, Winwick, £5 for the Whitecross Hospital, £5 for "Our Day" Fund, £25 for-the Prisoners of War FuAd. Parcels are Sent weekly to two old scholars who are prisoners of war in Westphalia'. The headmaster (Mr. G. W. A. Brown) and staff take this opportunity of thanking the parents for their generosity.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
One Of Four Soldier Sons Wounded

In The Leg.

Mrs. Smith, •of 1, Bennett-street, has been informed that one of her four soldier sons, Rifleman Joseph Smith, of the Rifle Brigade, has been wounded in the leg. Rifleman Smith, who is 20 years of age, enlisted on the outbreak of war, and had been in France twelve months. He formerly worked at Reynolds's Tannery, and was an old boy of St. Thomas' School, Stockton Heath. He has had a brother, Private Robert Smith, killed, as reported in , anothef-Qcolumn, and has two more brothers still serving, Private Charles Smith of the Grenadier Guards, and Sergeant John Smith of the Inniskilling Fusiliers.

RIFLEMAN JOHN FEENEY.

Received Bullet Wound In The Left

Iland.

Rifleman John Feeney, of the King's Royal Rifles, 44, Catherine-street, has been brought to Townley Hospital suffering from a bullet wound in the left hand, and he is going on well.

Rifleman Feeney, who has a wife and seven children, is 35 years of age, enlisted in Decem- ber, 1914, and has seen 14 months of active ser- vice. He was educated at St. Benedict's R.C. School, and was formerly employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd.

PRIVATE H. S. BAILEY.

One Of Three Soldier-Brothers Wounded.

- Private 11. S. Bailey, South Lancashire Regi- ment, whose home is at -6, Church-street, has been wounded in the right arm and shoulder.

Private Bailey, who formerly worked for Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and 'Sons, Ltd., is a son of Mr,. Bailey, of Stockton Heath, and is 32 years of age. He has played football for Warrington, Runcorn, Stockton Heath and Cadishead teams. He has two brothers also serving, Sergeant Clarence Bailey and Private Albert Bailey.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
PIM t;. A. APPLETON.

A Richmond's Employe Has A Hand Amputalvd At The Wrist.

The relatives of Private George Adam Appleton,, South Lancashire Regiment, who resides at 4, Hart's-place, off Bridge-street, have been officially informed that the Private has been wounded. His relatives went to see him on Sunday and found him going on as well as could be expected, he having had his hand amputated at his wrist. Writing from a Northampton hospital, a sister said that he was very cheerful and that she hoped he would soon be well again Private Appleton, who was called up under the Derby Group System. in February of this year, and had been in France five weeks, for- merly worked for Messrs. Richmond's Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd. He was educated at Heathside School, and has a brother in the A.S.C. and a cousin in the R.F.A.

"MAGGIE" MISSING.

Fruitless Search For A Beautiful

Warrington Girl.

EVERYONE INTERESTED.

" Maggie". remains undiscovered. In the official casualty. list she would be recorded as 'missing."

Last week-end, owing to the publication of her photograph in "The Examiner," "Maggie" was the talk of the town. It will be remem- bered that during a charge a dying comrade gave her photograph to Private Oldham, of 33, Ellesmere-street, and „asked him to give it to "Maggie, of Howley." Like Private Oldham, everyone joined in the search, but no one could locate her. Those fond of a pretty fate and the romantic 'constituted themselves into patrol parties in the hope of finding her, but all to no krpose. The latest report is "Still no news of her.",

Whether she ever graced a house in Howley has not yet been established. Had there been Women's Suffrage one might have been helped by the Voters' List. But that she is not in the district now is beyond doubt. With her face as the, target of every .eye, she could not move undetected.

Few people have refrained from looking tor .her. Hunting for a girl is invariably interest- ing, if she is good-looking like !` Maggie." It is much more exciting than following the hounds. Frequenters of the town have been most fervent in their devotions to her. Not a girl has passed by without being subjected to a facial scrutiny, which has proved fatal to the hope ,within them. Before the " Knuts " turned out 071 Saturday evening they were extra careful over their toilet, lest they should meet and their charms should not match her's. But the " Knuts" no more attracted, her to Market Gate than the policeman. She was °aside the orbit of their influence.

To everyone on Saturday the one question was, "lave you seen 'Maggie and in places where the people meet for converse and conviviality a toast to :the absent one was honoured. .Some vf the barmaids were re-christened -" Maggie," mid, glancing at her portrait Once more, gave the "long pull." for the compliment. - • • : It is not Our fault that she goes undiscovered.

One of our observers called at Private Old- ham's residence, 33, Ellesmere-street, yesterday- afternoon, to see if there had been any develop- ment. Re had hardly drummed his knuckles against the door when, one after another dozens of other doors in the street opened, sand the eyes of many ladies With Friday-afternoon- blacking-the-grate-appearances were directed towards No. 33 on. the chance of learning whether the interviewer had news of Iler. "So you haven't found her," remarked the lady of the house, after, in depressed, jilted-like tones, he had told of Ills failure. "Neithe'r have we," she added. "We are awfully disappointed. A lot of people have taken au,. interest in` the matter, and we have had several callers asking whether she has been heard of." In Ellesmere-street and district the story of the missing lady has caused much excitement. 'Every shop has been transformed into a con- sulting moth.. The most curious part about the whole affair is that not a few people fancy they have seen "Maggie." Perhaps the explanation is that it is a compliment to oneself to mention that one knows a pretty girl. We have a theory that Private Oldham mis- understood the directions of his dying comrade, who', after all, was in a Liverpool Regiment.

HIT IN A CHARGE.

Rifleman Parkinson Describes Experiences Graphically.

"A HELLISH TIME."

Said by an officer to have been in an action, as severe as any in the line, Rifleman Herbert Parkinson, son of Mr. and Mts. William Parkinson, 102, Bewsey-road,- who. now lies wounded in a Dublin hospital, has written a graphic letter relating his experiences. Rifleman Parkinson, who is 37 years of age,.

left Warrington in 1904 to take tip an impo-r-- ' tant appointment with the Surrey Education Committee. He is an Arpley-street "old boy" and before. securing an engagement at the Warrington Education Office was an assistant at the Museum and Library. He was also asso- ciated with Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, being a member of the choir, and was well- known in musigal circles generally, being at- tached to the Musical Society and • Male Voice Choir. His interest in the art never flagged, and at Kingston-on-Thames, where he resided, he took part in several operas, interpreting leading characters with conspicuous success. As a letter writer he: is excellent.

"I am so pleased to be able to let you know am still alive and kicking, although I have Lad a piece of iron or steel in ray head. One iece was taken out When I arrived at the

iressing station from the 'trenches (a piece of

my helmet) and the other piece (shrapnel) is still inside my knapper.' I expect to be oper- ated on next week, probably Tuesday, 26th inst., and then I shall be all right. "Well, I have had an experience' and no mistake. For many nights we had to walk four miles there and also four miles back from the trenches to assist the Canadians to repair

trenches and make dug-outs. It was there I really came under fire, and at first I confess I felt a little uncomfortable, but in time I got used to the whiz of the shells coming over, and the flying of shrapnel pll about. Several of our chaps got wounds, but I, was one of the lucky ones to come through scot free. It broke me in finely, and I was prepared afterwards for the hellish time.. that was to COW. , '

- - "We then had ,two. days in the train to an- other part of France, and we billeted at for two days. to give us a rest; then on we went for another long march up .to the reserve trenches.

"Then the ,business started in earnest 'We were in some old German trenches for the first ,night, waiting for morning, to march to the next trench. At midnight a shell or two came over just 50 yarOs from me, and buried ten Of our chaps, wounded eight and killed five. We dug out the ten, managing to get five out alive. We moved on in the morning and got to the

valley, which the men nick-named 'The Valley of Death,' and it was and no mistake! The English had pushed the Germans back abeut three miles two days before we arrived, and therefore 200 of us were detached to take water to our soldiers over the old battlefield. What a night! Although it was dark we could see the trenches were heaped up with German dead, some just coming out of dug-outs (I ex- pect they were killed by our.bombs) and others all lying over the fields where they' had fought their last. I was very glad to see they were Germans; in fact, the English dead were , very few.

"Well, we. went down a valley, up. another hill, right to the top of the crest: The Germans sent up their shells and we were subjected to • about 15 minutes' severe fire (shell and rifle). We all got in- shell holes and came out very fortunate, got the water tO the camp, and walked back as quickly as we could. • After• doing above for several nights and other work we gradually worked. our way up to the first , line trenches. It was hell, and no doubt. We got in the communication trench, but the Ger- mans discovered us. Then the shells came thick and half of were wiped out before we got to ,the wood where the trenches were. Up and down the trenches we were run until our 'chaps were in position, and then didn't we give them gip! "We took two trenches all Iright, and were just going over for the third time when a shell burst five yarcjs from me. I heard it coming and dropped on the ground to avoid the shrap- nel. It did not come off. I got a piece right through the back of •my helmet, knocking part tf it into my head, and then going into my napper,' and is still there. The blood spurted out of my head, down my face, and then I thought it time to get back to the. dressing station. When I came to myself I put a bandage on the wound, and then had to make a dash back over the trenches 'we had taken'. Shells and rifle fire were everywhere, but I had to risk it. I waited some time, and, when things eased down, jumped on top of the trench, 1, made a dash for the nearest 'shell hole, . and waited my opportunity. for, another dash. My heart was in my mouth, but I went on repeat- ing the job, ' until I got,/ to the ambulance. dug-out. I had my head dressed there and then, was told that all the stretcher-bearers were wiped out or had gone with other cases back beyond the fire zone. I said I would endeavour 'to go back by myself, so three of us walked in stages. It was three miles to go, and wasn't I glad to' get there! They were exceedingly kind to us, arid then I had, the piece of helmet taken out of my head, had it properly bandaged, and was put in an ambu- lance after ha,ving had something to eat.. By stages I •arrived :at Rouen Hospital and was: kept there for five days. They fed me -up (breakfast, porridge and eggs; dinner, chicken and rice; tea, bread ,and butter, jam, &c.; ,Supper. cocoa' and bread and butter) to get my strength back, gave me cigs. and grapes, &c., ' and we had a tip-top time I had may head X7rayed, and then made for `,Blighty;'. 'but' I• have arrived in Ireland. I am very hakppy here and well looked after. The Irish nurses are very- kind and have done all they can for 'us_ Of course ,the food'is plain, but good, and we shall not do badly 'on it."

" MISS9NG."

Suspense in The Case C Lance- Corporal KnoMes.

The uncertainty and suspense attaching to the fate of Lance-Corporal Robt. Parr Knowles, King's Liverpools, son Of Mr. W. Knowles, 14, Hewitt-street, is still further complicated this week by the receipt of a second official notifica- tion that lie has been "missing" since the engagement on the 30th July. It will be remembered he was first "unoffi- cially" reported killed on that day, which has been partially confirmed since by the return of letters -marked "Killed in action." But the War Office reports do not confirm this, their first report being that he waa" wounded" and the second one as "missing," both conveying a gleam of hope, which possibly may yet be realised.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
WERE ENGLAND FRANCE

WARRINGTON LILLE,

Why We Should All Join The

Volunteers.

By S.D.M.

Entering Bridge-street, Warrington, the other day,, my attention was arrested by a large poster which stands near the place where that memorable drawing of Nurse Cavell used to stand, forcibly calling upon all eligible War- rington men to avenge so ghastly a crime. This poster is of an entirely different nature, and simply bears the following message:— I t is the duty of every man at home to join the Volunteer*

How true! How clearly it tells us our duty. Although I have been a Volunteer for some considerable time, I honestly confess that until I read that poster I had not regarded my mem- bership of the Volunteers as seriously as I ought. I had gone to drill'time after time simply because I liked it, and not because it was my duty. Automatically, my mind seemed to travel to our dear lads out there, those dear brave lads who are fighting so treacherous an enemy and so bravely suffering the horrors of war, for you and me, for right against might./ Strange, But True.

Nine-tenths of the people at home who, through Tribunals, starred trades, over age, &c., have been exempted, are content to walk about doing nothing. What would you do if England were France, if Warrington were Lille? The horror of it! Warrington, our dear old mother town, in the occupation of so vile an enemy! Children torn from 'their mother's side; girls of tender ages sent to no one knows where; men going -about the streets carrying their lives in their hands, not daring to offer resistance, knowing too well the ghastly penalty, for a shot from a civilian may mean the whole- sale murder of the entire population; no one allowed to have a mouthful of food until the Kaiser's- gluttonous soldiers have had their fill. Fancy it. It seems like a horrible nightmare, but it's true, too true. That is the condition of things in Lille to-day, and the same would apply to Warrington, if England were-Northern Fran.-:e. The Fate of the Non-combatant.

I am not surmising for one moment that the same conditions will ever apply to us. God prevent it. But remember we-are still at •war. and the enemy we are up against will stop at nothing. Nothing is too low or too dirty for them that would assist them to accomplish their heart's desire, " the invasion of England.:' Thanks to our gallaht lads in blue it is almost an impossibility. Let it be truly recorded here, and impressed upon the minds of all liable men who chance to read this article, that in the event o fan invasion no one who is a non-combatant will be allowed to take up arms to defend that, and those he loves so dear. He will have to stand by and allow things to take their course. Naturally he may be called upon to do such work as digging trenches and burying dead, but under no conditions will he be allowed to take up arms. This is one of the outstanding conditions 4et down in the Hague convention, and England thinks a little too much of her honour to allow her subjects to break so sacred a vow.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Be' a Volunteer!

To remedy this and give every man who is, ineligible for his Majesty's regular forces a chance to do his bit, the Volunteer Training Corps have been thrown open to all. By taking a solemn vow to bear true allegiance a civilian instantly becomes a soldier, he is given all the advantages of a soldier of the line. By sacrificing an hour or two each week he will be able to prepare himself, and if the worst comes to the worst he will know how to act. Let the men of Lymm—and Warrington—respond as eagerly to this appeal as the youth of Lynim— and Warrington—did to that of the late Lord Kitchener.

LAY MM

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

The allied feeling i as keen in the workshop as on the battlefield. .

A Belgian refugee was this week the recipient of a beautiful silver cigarette case as a token of esteem from his fellow-employes at a local munition works.

He left them to join the Belgian Army.

As-I anticipated last week, the soldiers' con- cert went off Al.

I . People came from far and ,ne'ar to witness what proved to be a most extraordinary enter- tainment.

Even the good old " Examiner " landed in the first line of trenches.

• "Our hospital," plus the author's vivid im- agination, was one of the principal attractions.

Sergeant coreoran made a fine hit as the Matron. •

His acting was splendid, and the part he was called to play suited him down to the ground.

Corporal Heath, as the Doetor,*exe.lle.d him- self, and his quiet way of turning the patients over to the matron's own kind jutlgment caused endless rounds of laughter.

Private Fairfield, as, Jerry, was here, there, and everywhere, and certainly was the most popular comedian in the cast. .

His sly way of making lore to the nurse- girl fairly tickled the enthusiastic audience.

The splendid result was due to the splendid management; every soldier had his own par- ticular work to do, from stage manager to call boy.

The matron herself deserves a deal of credit, for the entire show came Under her own able management.

What did the Sergeant Matron mean when he advised his patient to be very careful about choosing their company?

And his continual remarks about the seat round the corner?

I hear that there is to be a repeat perform- ance this afternoon for the benefit of school children.

Three Septembers! What a coincidence

A local soldier reported wounded this week, enlisted in September, 1914, drafted out to France in September, 1915, and returns to Englancl-Wounded, in September, 1916.

A JOY RIDE

With Unpleasant Results: Police Court Sequel.

The Hon. J. E. Cross (chairman), Xice- Chancell6r Dudley Stewart Smith, K.C., Messm John Coxon, H. Holden and F. Lockyer, magis- trates sitting at Lynam on Wednesday, heard a 'prosecution under the Licensing Act which was somewhat unusual in its nature.

Two men hailing from Altrincham, named Harvy Arnold, painter, and William Jackson, labourer, were summoned by Constable Bison far being drunk on licensed premises on the Bth September. Defendants denied the charge. Inspector Brewster prosecuted.

_ Mr. Joseph C. Gough, licen.ee or The Jolly Thresher, Lymm, stated that at about 8.50 p.m. a taxi drove up to the door and the driver and Arnold came into the house. They were both drunk, and witness absolutely refused to serve them. They refused to go out, and said hey should have a drink, or tobacco or something. The taxi driver went out, but Arnold refused to do so, and was abusive. Witness went to get a police officer, and when he returned Arnold had gone out by the back way. Jackson was in the car and witness could not

Arnold: All I said to you was, "That is how you treat wounded soldiers."

- Witness stated that Arnola said he had been fighting at Mons or somewhere. He called wit- ness a German and said he should be under the sod.

Mrs. Gough corroborated her husband as to Arnold's condition. The other defendant, Jack- son, also came in and asked, for a glass of whisky and soda, which she would not serve him because he was drunk. She told him to go out and he did so. The two defendants had gone away when the policeman arrived. Inspector Brewster said this affair ,seemed to have been the result of a joy ride. The taxi driver was locked up and charged with being drunk while in charge of a motor car. He was fined 205., and the magistrates expressed the opinion that these two men should also be brought before the Bench. Arnold said he was not drunk on that occasion. He had been invited for a drive by Jackson, who was going to see his sister. When he saw the condition of the driver he refused to go further in the car. He walked to Heatley Station and got the train home. Mrs. Arnold stated that her husband got home about 1,0.30. He was not drunk.

Insperetor Brewster: Did he tell you that Jackson had a bottle of whisky in the motor car?—Yes, and that he gave the motor driver a glass of it. Jackson also denied having been drunk on the occasion., When he was refused a drink he walked out immediately. • The magistrates fined each of the defendant, 10s. and ss. costs.

THE ALLIED FEELING.

Presentation To Belgian Refugee

Called To The Colours.

A pleasing ceremony took plqee at the Lanca- shire Tool Works, Henry-street, on Tuesday afternoon, when Mr. Andrew Van Der Mens- brougghs, a Belgian refugee, was presented with a silver cigarette case by his fellow employes. Mr. Andrew Van Der Mans- brougghs tells a remarkable tale. His home, he says, is in a Belgian town which at present is in the occui)ation of the enemy. His parents are still there, and he has not heard from them for months. When the Germans first entered his native town he was advised to get away, and after considerable difficulty he was able to get over to England. He came to Oughtrington and secured a situation with Messrs. Welford and -Co. at Broomedge, afterwards securing I) good berth with, Messrs. Wm. Moss and Sdns, of the Lancashire Tool Works. He has just turned 18 years of age, and has been called to the Belgian colours, and left Lymm to undergo his medical test in London on Wednesday. morn- ing.

-Everything was going on as usual oui Tuekday afternoon, when suddenly the large gas engine which works all the tnachinery ceased, work was stopped, and all the employes were quietly told to assemble in one of the large store rooms upstairs. When the excitement had died down Mr. Albert Brown, one of the employes, handed their Belgian workmate a beautiful silver cigarette case as a token of good feeling from his fellow employes. Ve remarked that they were all sorry to lose him, as he was a good workmate, but they were plea.sea to know that he was goinkc to fight for the Allies' noble cause of right against that military machine which represented nothing but might. (Ap- plause.) Replying, the recipient said, "I am too full to say much, but I thank you from the bottom of my heart. I have tried to do my bit here making munitions, but now my time has come, and I am pleased to be able to go and fight side by side with the British ,Army, and to help to bring this terrible war to an early finish."

•The pleasing ceremony then concluded with the singing of "For he's a jolly good fellow," which re-echoed through the whole village.

THREE SEPTEMBERS.

Extraordinary Coincidence Relating To

Local Soldier..

News has this week been received to the effect that Private Percy, Appleton, the Cheshire Regiment, has been wounded in the left arm. Writing to his sister in Statham, the gallant soldier says: "Here we are again, still aliVe and kicking. No doubt you will be surprised to learn that I have landed in 'Blighty' with a nice one through the muscle of the left arm. The shrapnel went in the muscle and came out through my elbow. Now. that I am able to get up and knock about I would not part with it for a quid, although it has been 'some sore.' I have been under two operations, but it is now Al. It is a treat being in hospital and 'not half a nice warm bed; and what a relief to get rid of 'those visitors.' The only drawback, is that it is a war hospital and we don't get those

Wounded

extras, but never mind, we manage to get a packet of cigarettes a week. Oh! but it is a nice one, and the nurse is just coming to dress it. It is Hell on eaPth on the Somme front."

This gallant soldier enlisted in September, 1914, was drafted out to France in September, 1915, and returned to .England wounded in September, 1916. He is 22 years of age, and was educated at Lymm School, and worshipped at the Primitive Methodist Church. He resides with his father at Heatley, and prior to the war was employed as a fitter by Chas. Moore Erni Co., salt manufacturers, Heatley. He is at present in the Northumberland War Hospital, N 'wcastle-On-Tyne. •

RED CROSS CONCERT.

Wounded Soldiers As Authors And Entertainers.

Times without number we have been treated to concerts, garden fetes and similar pleasures in aid of our' Brookfield Hospital, but last Wednesday's effort was of an entirely different nature. It was a concert given in aid of our Brookfield Hospital by the wounded soldiers themselves„ Nor has there ever been an enter- tainment given to an appreciative audience which contained more artistic talent than that given to the exceedingly large audience that gathered on this occasion. Every item on the programme was of "tip-top" excellence, and well deserved the thunderous rounds ..f applause that almost lifted the roof of the large building. The introduction was made by Private Fair- field, the author and producer of the two sketches, who asked the audience not to be too Yard on the artistes, as they had had little time in which to practise, and if they happened to make any mistakes [which they didn't] would the company treat them as lightly as possible? Then came the following splendid pro- gramme :—Chorus, "My old "Kentucky home," " Tennessee," Gunner Montague; comic, In which figured the renowned cinema artiste. "Charlie Chaplin," Gunner Gibbons and Private Bartlett; recitation, " Hursley Camp," Private Jeffery; "Mandalay," Private Fair- field; " Which swithch, &c." Private Davies; [" The Trumpeter," Private Davidson; "Micky Dun's Party," Private Piggott; "Come, sing to me," Private Cooper; recitation, Private Jeffery; "Long, long trail," Gunner Peck. " Our Hospital." Then came the first of Private Fairfield's remarkable sketches, "Our hospital," in two scenes. The characters w-?.re: Geo. Parker, Gunner Gibbons; Jerry Knowles, Private Fair- field; Matron, Sergeant Corcoran; Red Cross Nurse, Private Holt; Doctor, Corporal Heath; Mrs. Wells, Private Piggott; Baby, Private Kentish; Nursemaid, Private Davis; Patients, Privates Jeffery, Cooper and Taylor. The first scene was the kitchen of Mrs. Parker's resi- dence (the hospital washerwoman), in which some amusing cross-talk was obtained from Geo. Parker and Jerry Knowles, chiefly con- cerning the hospital round the corner, the follies of women, nurses working for nought, and the seats in the new Lever-road. The second scene represented a ward in the Brook- field Hospital. The matron (Sergeant Cor- coran) is busy putting the patients through their early morning test (and torture), when somehow or other Geo. • Parker and Jerry Knowles get mixed with the patients and, though loudly protesting, they are placed in the fatal chair and put through a series of tor- tures. Then comes the doctor (Corporal Heath), who again puts them through it, and adds insult to injury by putting them all, on special (very special) diets. He also confines them to hospital for the day, and the' matron gives them some sound advice about the hos- pital's good name, keeping their ties straight, choosing their company, and concludes by tell- ing them that we want "none of their Winwick tricks here." Meanwhile the baby sitting in a bassinette is vigorously protesting, and creates considerable amusement. That con- cluded the first sketch, cleverly written and brilliantly acted. When \the curtain rose at the commencement of the second half of the programme it could be noticed that the scenery had undergone a sudden transformation and now represented the centre of a large wood. The second half com- menced with a quartet and was followed by : Song, "Major General Worthington," by Private Davis; "Genevieve," Private Piggott; comic, Gunner Gibbons; "The Broken Doll," Gunneralontague; " To-morrow will be Fri- day," Private Fairfield-; "Mountains of Mourne," Private Davidson; "Polly Wolley Doodle" (with chorus), Private Cooper; "When I lost you," Private Peck. Lymm News "Somewhere in France."

The second sketch was "Somewhere in France." The scene represented a portion of the first•line of trenches. A party of English Tommies" were waiting to be relieve& seated round a cokp fire, when suddenly the Sergeant enters and informs them that they are shortly to be relieved by Captain Williamson and his Cheshire Volunteers. (Applause.) Then comes the rum ration, followed by the chorus "If the Sergeant drinks your rum, never mind," and "Baby poll." Then the orderly goes out for rations, but manages to get hit with a Maxim gun round the corner and is brought in by the stretcher-bearers with a nice "Blighty one" in the arm. Then there is trouble about another ration orderly to take the place of the one wounded, which ends up with the chorus "Pack all your troubles in your old kit-bag." Then the postman. arrives with a:parcel bearing the Lymm postmark for "Jerry," and a sheaf of letters. The parcel is immediately opened and the first article taken out is an English paper, "The Warrington Examiner." (Loud applause.) ".Good old Lymm! What's the Lymm News?" (Renewed applause.) The Sergeant gets a letter from Cherry Cottage, Cherry-lane, which turns out to be a love letter, and as he finishes reading it the soldiers 'sing "Are we downhearted?" This is followed by the "Old folks at home," and as the soldiers fall asleep "Beep the home fires burning" could be heard in the distance. The finishing touches were given by the rattle of a Maxim gun and the boom of a big gun behind the scenes. - Praise for the Workers.

During a break in the programme, Mr. A. de C. Parmiter, a member of the General Committee, said no Lymm entertainers had ever received thanks more cordial and sincere than those they would give the soldiers that evening. The idea of the concert origin- ated from them, which spoke very well for the hospital. Nurse Morehouse, the pianist, was on night -duty at the hospital, but she had sacrificed her sleep in the daytime to enable tte, men to -practise. (Applause.) Next came Sergeant Corcoran, the heart of the whole thing and orgauiser-in-chief—(renewed ap- plause)—who would be feeling very pleased at the success of his efforts. Private Fairfield's two sketches were cleverly written and splen- didly acted. "It is a pity he has not given us a representation of what he thinks the General Committee is like," said the speaker', amid laughter. "Now I will ask you to give a hearty round/ of applause for the soldiers' splendid effort." The applause was duly given.

Result.

The proceeds of the concert amounted to .237 10s. 91d.

A POSER FOR THE BENCH.

A Difference Between Cigarettes And

Tobacco.

At Lymm Petty Sessions James Shepherd, a local tobacconist, having his premises in Bridgewater-street, was summoned by Sergeant Griffiths for having, on the 20th August, sold cigarettes to a boy named Hatton, who was under the age of 16 years. _

The Sergeant stated that he saw four boys, of whom Hatton was one, and who were all under the age of 16 years, smoking cigarettes in Back- lane.

Defendant admitted having sold the cigarettes, but stated that the boy, said they were for a young man outside. Defendant said that Mrs. Shepherd desired to know if a gentleman sent Et. boy under 16 years for his tobacco was she to 'serve him.

The question occasioned much merriment amongst the magistrates, and the Act of Par- liament was at once requisitioned. It was then discovered that the clause in the Children's Act only binned " cigarettes " and "'cigarette papers." .

1- - The Chairman (Hon J. E. Cross) asked Inspector Brewster if this was the first case of the kind in Lymm, and the latter replied that it was to his knowledge. The Chairman said 'defen4ant hao,asked a question and raised a point that had given the Bench much amusement. The Act only applied to cigarettes and cigarette papers, and, there- fore, they should not ,have been sold to the boy. As it was elle first case of the kind in -Lynam defendant would be fined only one shilling. '

Dear Apples: Warrington Boys .Charged With Theft:A-At the Juvenile Court, which sat at the Council Offices on Wednesday morning, three Wllrrington boys came before Messrs. T. J. Ridgway and H. Holden, charged with steal- ing , 71b. of apples, the property of Mr. Peter Hankey, of Thelwall. Defendants were found guilty and were fined .55., 3s. 6d. and 2s. 6d. respectively. These petty thefts are getting quite 'a common occurrence, and it is hoped thee(' summonses will be a warning to others.

THE LATE MR. A. W. CALLENDER'S WILL.

The will of Mr. A. W. Callender, of Lymm, has been proved at £10,265.

Stack Fire At Rettclish.—Last Thursday after- noon a serious fire broke out in the stack yard of Reddish Hall }arm, Reddish, the property of Messrs. R. and W. Heatheote. A large stack of hay weighing about 35 tons caught fire and was burned to the ground. The alarm was immediately given and the Lymm Fire Brigade, under Captain Wm. Rose, were soon on the scene, but the fire was then beyond con- trol. They succeeded in preventing the fire from spreading to two other stacks which were close at hand.

PADGATE.

Private Scott Ward.—We are pleased to • re- port that Privates Scott Ward, of Cinnamon- lane, Fearnhead, who has been removed from France to King George's Trospital, London, is progressing favourably. He was visiteil by his mother and sister on Sunday, and they were agreeably surplised to find him so cheerful and comfortable.

Works Picnic.—Owing to the kindness of Mr. Edward Gorton, the employes of the Padding- ton Bone Works had a very enjoyable outing to Southport last S4urday. The party left Padgate in the morning in two saloon coaches, special facilities for stopping express trains for them having been arranged by the railway com- pany both on the outward and return journeys. The firm very kindly provided railway fare and dinner, and,the day turned out very fine, which added much to the enjoyment. Mr. W. Jump (secretary) and Mr. H. Stafford (foreman), accompanied the party. Women's Missionary M eeting.—The quarterly meeting under the auspices of the Women's Auxiliary was held in the Padgate Wesleyan Sunday School on 'Thursday afternoon, presided over by Miss Turner, the Vicarage. A very in- teresting address was given by Miss Lowen- stein, of Birmingham, organising secretary of the WOmen's Auxiliary, in which she outlined various methods of raising funds to help on the good work being done amongst the Zenanas. She also made a strong appeal for helpers to carry on the work, and explained the course of training which was necessary in order to be- come a missionary worker. Mrs. Cottrell, of Fearnhead, was the soloist, and she also gave P sweet rendering of the solo, "I gave My life for thee!' Miss Turner and the speaker were given a hearty vote of thanks. A collection taken in aid of the society realised £5 10s. 6d. There were a goofl number present, and after the meeting tea was provided at a small cost.

Church Lads' Brigade.—A concert organised by the members of the Church Lads' Brigade was held in the C.E. Schools on Thursday even- ing. Recently this brigade became affiliated with the Cadet Corps, and the members- are now wearing khaki uniforms, and the iponcert was promoted to defray the expenses of the new equipment, as well as to raise funds for the further development of the organisation. There was a good atray of talent on the programme, and judging by the numerous encores the selections were suited to the taste of the audience. Mr. Poole was 'very successful in his songs, "Drake goes west" and "Up from Zummerzet," and Private Young sang in a spirited manner "Sunshine of your smile." Mr. Sephton sang "Maisie, my girl" and "The trumpeter," whilst Mrs. Sephton was heard in the old favourite, a Angus Macdonald." Miss Hewitt, who is always a favourite with Padgate audiences, sang in her usual finished style, "When the heart is young," also joining in the well-known duet, "Excelsior' along with Mr. Poole. A duet• entitled " Springtime " was given by Mr. and Mrs. Sephton. "Watchman, what of the night?" by Messrs. SephtOn and Poole, and a humorous item, "Don't we, Harold," by Messrs. Beswick and Berry. A. humorous trio, "The ideal home," was given by Messrs. Beswick, Berry and Harrison, and the quartets, "When the budding bloom" and "Anchored"• were also sung by Members of the concert party. Mr. Harrison created much amusement by his humorous selections, " P.C. 49" and "Phil the fluter's ball." There were a good number present, the chairman being Mr. George Proffitt, of Padgate.

SHORTAGE OF MEN AND LEATHER.

Relative to the unbadging of men in the trade (says the Warrington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review ") there have been several interesting appeals made locally during the past week, and whilst in some cases mutual arrangements have been made 'at the Tribunals to allow a proportion of the men to remain in their employment, others are held in abeyance pending further inquiries. There is no doubt that this depletion of labour will cause much inconvenience at a time like this (especially when all those who have been released have joined up), as in face of the fresh demands of the War Office for leather of all classes, the trade could do with more men and not less, and as there is practically no more substitution to be got, there•will,be great difficulty in combating the requirements. On top of this there is a likelihood, of further combing out, and„ as pointed out in these columns a few weeks ago, there will be nothing for it but reduced inputs.

A feeling was prevalent in certain quarters that a proportion of bends would be released for the civilian trade towards October, but there seems very little hope of this, considering the numbers that are still required by the Army authorities. - The local representative of the "Leather World" says : Tanners have now information before them of the requirements of the Government of the different kinds of leather and the apprdximate quantities to be provided up to the end of the year. It is certainly an enormous amount, and covers the Italian boot order, a further million pairs of boots for the Russian Government, bends for repairing Army boots, and the upper ad sale leather for the service boots for our own Army. Whilst tanners can sell through factors with the guarantee that the leather will be used for purposes approved by the Director of Army Contracts after October, this is, of course, no concession for boot manufacturers for domestic purposes, who will evidently have te• rely on imported leather absolutely, as, in. .order to' fill Government requirements, practi- cally all the leather produced in this country will be earmarked down to 61b. bends.

Unwise habits load the blood with uric poisons. The kidneys act as safety-valves, filtering the• blood and passing off the poisons, but are bound to weaken under any long continued strain, and, once they falter, you feel dullvtired and nervous. Your back aches, you lose weight, you have headaches, dizzy spells and urinary disorders.
