BOWLING.

"GEORGE AND TOSS."

Veterans Start Charity Bowling

Tournament.

Still another charitable effort was favoured with magnificent weather when an extremely interesting bowling match between Lancashire Professionals and Warrington Aniateurs came off on Saturday at Rylands- ntothers' Recrea- tion Ground.

Although the afternoon became rather chilly, the opening stages of the contest were conducted under, extremely pleasant conditions and brilliant sunshine was a prominent factor in attracting hundreds to the fixture. The proceeds being in aid of local charitable insti- tutions, a great debt is due to that excellent sportsman, Mr. William Green, jun., who not only got the professionals together, but de- frayed their expenses, and, in addition, gave a handsome donation. The ground was in splen- did trim, and although plenty of rain fell the previous day, the green where the matches were played offered every inducement for clever play. Standing and seating accommoda- tion had been provided through the kindness of Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., and it was estimated that between five and six hundred persons were present when the pro- gramme opened. The arrangements were ad- mirable, under the direction of Mr. J. Wood, hon. secretary. Mr. F. Dean was referee, and Messrs. T. Pa4tington kBlackliool) and T. Garnett acted as measurers.

It was a great pity that the silver band of the R.A.0.8. was not present to play on those grand old exponents of the game, W. Taylor (" Owd Toss ") and George Ball, sen., who headed the programme and had uninterrupted use of the green for fifteen minutes, before a later generation were permitted to enter the lists. It .was pre-eminently an occasion for a blare of trumpets, and "See the conquering heroes come."

The Psalmist's limit pf three score years and ten has been well exceeded by these doughty veterans, for they are both, to use a nautical phrase, "Seventy-fours." Taylor had the advantage in age—he is a month older than Ball; but the latter had the advantage in bowls, because he won the game. Both the old men were in the best of spirits, and replied to the hearty reception of the crowd with Some ancient quips, which, apparently, lost none of their pun- gency on the present occasion. Starting from the centre of the green, they gradually lengthened their distances, until they finally "showed off" with long corner shots. They were pretty stiff at first setting out, but as they warmed to the game and got exhilarated with the onlookers' applause., they followed the jack with a wonderful amount of energy. , There was a deal of arm waving, urging and demonstrat- ing as each of the aged players tried to give his opponent the inside crook, "Good shot, Toss !" "Well done, George !" was the order of the day, and excitement ran high when the game wa level at nineteen up. The veterans had worried each other's flanks all the time, and when a tremendous effort by Ball got "two in" and completed the victorious figure twenty- one, there was a long round of hearty applause. It was a notable conflict—a tough, hard-won fight. _ _

The match resulted in an extremely credit- able win for the amateurs by 33 points. Appended is the list of players and scores:—

BY ALEC J. BRAID.

sectors in this story are purely Imaginary living

CHAPTER XVII.

THE SING OF DIAMONDS AND THE QUEEN OF HEARTS. When Esther had sufficiently recovered from her 'exciting experience she and Quabba bade farewell to the kind-hearted mine boss and his wife. "You Will find Mr. Powell on his yacht at Santa Barbara." they were told, and in a trap placed at their disposal they started for the railroad thirty miles away. The driver's face was wreathed with a cheerful smile of appreciation at the honour he felt was his in convoying so fair a young woman and so interesting a personage as the foreign droll, Quabba, ensconced on the back. Esther listened to the driver with seeming atten- tion, but her thoughts were far away. The old feud of the stanleighs would not die down. Girl as she was, Esther felt inspired with blood hatred, Which is as strong as blood's more kindlier ties, and stronger. Blair was in, her thoughts, more in Contempt than fear, while of his gipsy companion and accomplice, Luke Lovell, she thought not at all. As the trap sped along towards the base of the mountain, Blair Stanleigh and Luke Lovell had gained the summit in their long and arduous tramp from the mine portal to • the other side of the range. , Blair's field-glasses were focussed upon Esther, it would seem, at the very moment her mind's eye Was focussed upon him. "There goes Esther," cried Blair, putting down the field-glasses and pointing to the trap that Moved, a mere speck to the naked eye, along the road far down below. "We can intercept her yet 117 dropping straight down this cursedly steep biasuntain," Blair added. Luke grunted a surly assent, and the two uesPerate adventurers started down the rough, straight mou.ntain trail at as brisk a pace as they dared attempt. That Arthur Stanleigh was or was hot what he seemed was of little moment to Luke. Ile was bewildered on the edge of a great secret of Which he had but the faintest glimpse and grasp. only remembered that Matt Harding, Hagar's 'hug dead husband, had trafficked with the gentle- folk of Stanleigh Hall and had made his gipsy fortune thereby. Luke also knew that Esther had the document that was the essence of this Stanleigh secret. It also concerned The Diamond from the Sky. Luke had no love for Blair Stanleigh. He determined ,ur.on two things. One was that no harm should befall Esther, for whom Luke had long held a wild affection. The other resolve concerned the dia- mond. Seemingly the accomplice of Blair Stan- leigh in his tiesperate schemes to regain and Possess it, yet in the back of his mind he felt his deepest joy would be when he throttled Blair Stanleigh and tore it from his weakening grasp. As Blair and Luke sped_ down the mountain, over a rocky and half-obliterated trail, to intercept Esther, liarmaduke Smythe, guiding himself by the sun, plodded along the road at the base of the mountain. Marmadnke was imbued with one idea. The idea was that "England, home, and beauty," as he expressed it to himself, lay to the east. So at sunrise he faced the east, carrying the suit case, his gun, and the deer head, which more and more he had come to believe was a bona- fide trophy of his prowess as a hunter in the American jungle. The Diamond from the Sky Meant little or nothing to Marmaduke Smythe. " How it came where found I cannot for the life hf me imagine," he mused. "I only know, Mar- Inaduke Smythe, you are a jolly fool to have ,ever left England to find the Yankee heir to the Stan- leigh Earldora." These reveries were ended abruptly by a succes- ion of pistol shots. The startled lawyer next heard a pounding clatter of hoofs and the rattle of wheels coming behind him. Then he felt him- self bowled over and scattered with his belongings dust. Picking himself up, he saw that the wagon was driven by the pretty girl who had shared the Coach with him just previous to the wreck that had made him what he was now—a wanderer in the wilderness. But hardships and adventure had sharpened the wits of the timid lawyer. He gave a willing hand to help the horses out of their tangle of harness against the hillside, and was quick to accept the • proffer of a lift from the Mysterious young woman who had been his fellow- •.bassenger in the wrecked coach and the strange- looking hunchback with her. Luke and Blair had failed again. They had intercepted the trap, and had shot down the brave young miner, who had refused to halt at their sudden shouts to do so when they had sprung out from the brush of the hillside on to the road. "They have shot me! Drive on and save your- selves," had been the last words of the driver, and even as he had fallen, or thrown himself from the seat, he had struck the horses a quick, heavy blow with the whip. The maddened horses left the Pursuing Luke and Blair far behind. At a fork in the road Esther took the wrong turn and left the road to the rail. Realising this, they waited till daybreak. Around their small camp fire the three so strangely thrown together recounted the eventful" adventures that had befallen them since the wrecking of the coach. 'lt is a coincidence, my dear young lady," Ifarmaduke Smythe remarked finally, "that you are seeking for this elusive young millionaire, John Powell, too. I have a letter for him- from that astute chap Blake, the Richmond detective. This slake is a most reticent person, but he assured ..'ne that if I came to California and saw this John tiowell, the latter would help me to locate Arthur Stanleigh. Whether he is a ticket of leave man 'r is to be lynched or remain as a fugitive is not 'll my province to argue. He is the heir to the Stanleigh Earldom. But frankly, young lady, if Yigland expects me to go any further in doing my duty in this matter, England is vastly mistaken. I am going back as soon as I can find a train and boat to take me. If you are seeking this John 4"owell also, will you ask him to convey this to Arthur Stanleigh? Oddly enough. although I was not at all surprised at anything that happened to ' hie in America, I found this yesterday." To the breathless amazement of Esther and 9uabba, the lawyer brought from his pocket, in the Most matter of fact manner, The Diamond from the Sky. Neither the girl nor her hunch- -IMcfc. r)rr, reer)seeer, ceecerxr.

0

and have no connection with or relation to any person.l

back protector could grasp or solve the mystery of the strange reappearance of the great and ancient Stanleigh heirloom here in this wild land. As .for the lawyer, he only asked to be rid of it. "I wish no further /responsibilities, and I would not stay in the jungles of America for a leather hatbox full of diamonds from the sky," he added. "So please deliver it to 'Mr. Powell' for Arthur Stanleigh, with my compliments, exacting, of course, a receipt." As he said the words he made a brief receipt in his notebook and tendered the book and the fountain pen to Esther, adding, "Please sign, Miss Harding." But when Esther affixed her name boldly, "Esther Stanleigh," the English lawyer scratched his head in a puzzled manner. But although he was puzzled he was not surprised. Nothing that could now happen in America would surprise him. Esther clasped the diamond around her neck. by every right and title it belonged to her. The impulse that had actuated her father, the late Colonel Stanleigh, to rob her of her birthright in order that the great gem might never go to Blair Stanleigh, his enemy's child, was the impulse that made Esther's 'heart beat resolutely with the same resolve. If The Diamond from the Sky was in sooth the Stanleigh tarm against harm, Esther Stanleigh needed its protecting influence now. Later in the day they reached the railroad and travelled to Santa Barbara.

Called by business to Los Angeles, Arthur in- vited Vivian Marston to accompany him. In that city she was weaving the spell of her /charms about the rich young man. Her task was not so easy as she imagined it would be. ~Arthur's heart was with Esther, and he longed to see the sweet girl and pour out the story of his love and hopes. It was because of this that Vivian did not find Arthrr such an easy victim as she did Blair, and the young woman, eager to possess the diamond, could not understand Arthurts attitude.

Seated in the garden of her hotel and planning the future, Vivian was astonished when a dapper young Frenchman, attired in the height of fashion, claimed acquaintanceship. In response to her denials, he deftly placed a card in her hand and was gone.

Vic-lan had recognised de Vain, and was almost afraid to look at the card he had thrust upon her. The card was a playing card, the king of dia- monds! "What can Durand want?" she whis- pered to herself as she Quietly walked back to the hotel.

In the privacy of her room she split the card to find the message. If the message was genuine she knew where to find it. There it was, with all its probabilities. "To the Queen of Hearts.—You will work with us. We, too, are after The Diamond from the Sky.—The King of Diamonds." No, there was no mistake. The past had come back to strike her a cruel blow. With a run the walls of the castle she had been rearing crumbled to the ground. Clever schemer though she was, Vivian Marston was not a match for Frank Durand, and she realised that now he was after the jewel, if the gem were discovered it would be sold and the proceeds divided as mere plunder. The King of Diamonds had no sentiment in his make-up. He was all the time out for business, and the recovery of the Sta,nleigh "charm against harm" was to him merely a business proposition. Never would she possess the jewel she had schemed so much to gain. She found, too, that, old ties were not easily broken. When Vivian met Durand she tried to bluff him. "That won't do, Vivian," he replied. ." You have hidden yourself successfully for many months, but we are not through with you, my dear girl. We must find the diamond, and share and share alike. You tell me you don't know where it Is. Flatly, I don't believe it. I insist that you help us. You dare not defy me 1" And Vivian knew that Durand spoke the truth.

Having lost Esther and the document, Blair re- turned to Santa Barbara and wired to Los Angeles to Arthur, who, at the instigation of Vivian, by telegram instructed the sailing master to allow Blair to take charge. "Make 'yourself at home, Mr. Stanleigh," said the ,sailing master, when Blair went aboard. "You will find things in Mr. Powell's cabin." " "I will need a good deal of fixing up," Blair explained, laughingly. "My man and I got lost in the mountains while hunting," and he indicated Luke, who had come aboard with him. "Let your man go to the forecastle, and I will see he is fitted out," said the sailing master. "As for you, sir, I take it from Mr. Powell's telegram that you are to help yourself to what you wish of his things until you are sent for." While Blair was making himself presentable in Arthur's cabin, Esther, accompanied by Quabba, was making way across the • harbour in the hope of finding Arthur on the yacht. Arriving by the companion-way, Esther cautioned Quabba to make no explanation if questioned, as she wished to, give Arthur the happy surprise of facing him unannounced. But no sailor on watch barred her way, nor were any questions shouted over the rail to Quabba. Esther, crossing the deck, saw no sailor or officer in charge. She heard someone stirring abaft, and, in what was evidently the owner's cabin, she noted a tall and stalwart young man in uniform, his back turned to' the open door. Esther tiptoed forward, and, pulling The Diamond from the Sky out in view upon her neck, her lips framed the word "Arthur," when she found herself gazing horrified into a mirror that reflected the handsome but wicked face of Blair.

Esther turned with a cry for help, and sped across the doorway to the forecastle. The bulky form of a leering, sodden brute creature faced her. It was Luke Lovell. Esther turned and darted to one side, missing ,the clutching grasp of Blair Stanleigh by a hair's-breadth. The sudden revul- sion from hope and happiness to fear and despair had driven Esther to a frenzy of sudden fright. As though upon the wings of fear, he fled. Hardly knowing what she did save that she escaped her enemies, Esther plunged over the sheer side of the yacht into the sea! (To be continued.) >recerrAceeercereeeCee"ni

HUMANITY SAVED AT VERDUN.

Mr. Lloyd George's Glow ing Praise

Of Prance.

The following official account is published of banquet given in honour of Mr. Lloyd George VI Friday in the 'casements of the citadel of lerdun, • to which he was conducted by M. Albert Thomas, Minister of Munitions, and ?eneral Dubois. After dinner General Dubois !I_3r.ilg proposed the toast of the distinguished visitor and the great allied nation, Mr. Lloyd George replied as follows :—I wish YO of all, General, to tell you of the pleasure -,1111.11 have given me by asking me to sit at table with Your officers in the very heart of your those i& adel of Verdun. I a happy to see around :Lose who have come from battle and those "na will be in it to-morrow and those who mount guard with you on these. inviolable The name of Verdun will awaken in tlfiltul7e ages an imperishable memory. 'None of great feat of arms with which ,the history France is filled has better displayed the pleheat qualities of the French Army and i.rench nation, and this bravery and this devo- :,(ln to the Fatherland, to Which the world has 'ways paid homage, have been reinforced by a

coolness and tenacity that need not fear com- parison with the British phlegm_ , The memory of the 'victorious resistance ,at Verdun will be immortal, because Verdun has saved not only France lint our great common cause and all humanity. On the heights which surround this old citadel the maleficent power ,of the enemy has been, 'broken like a furious sea on a granite rock. They have tamed the storm which,menaced the world. For my own part I feel profound emotion on treading this sacred soil. I. do not speak only for myself, but bring you the heartfelt admiration of my country and the great Empire which I repre- sent here. They bow like myself before the sacrifices and glory of Verdun. Once more humanity turns to France for the defence of the great causes with which its whole future is bound up. - .

In the midst of deep silence Mr. Lloyd George then raised his glass and said :—" To 'France, to the heroes and the graves of Verdun!"

INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

A FAMOUS 22.

Ileathside Make Rings Round Beamont.

The opening mateh in the loeal Intermediate League was played on the Wilderspool ground on Saturday, Heathside-and. Beamont being the opposing teams.

Beamont were not at full strength, but, even if they had been, it is questionable whether the result would have been much different. Heathside were a bigger and speedier set, and flung the ball about in no half-hearted manner. Nine times in ten the ball came out of the " scrum " to the Heathside backs, with the re- sult that the opposition was often left guess- ing. Heathside's victory of twenty-two points to nil was well deserved, and it would be un- fair to individualise their players, for all did well.

For the losing team, Whit&ld worked like a, trojan and saved many difficult situations. He tackled keenly and kicked with great power. On the' right wing, Barlow did many good things, and Pembrton was the pick of the forwards.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PLAY.

Heathside won the toss and elected to play with the sun at their backs. Pemberton started for Beamont, and the firit incident of note was a pass- ing .movement by the Heathside backs, which took them to the Beamont quarters. Here, however, they were penalised, and driven back to midfield. After some even play Forster received for Heath- side, and raced along the touch-line, but in attempt- ing to kick sent ,the ball out of play. From the scrummage the Heathside backs received and worked their way to the Beamont line. Here—some exciting play was witnessed, two or three scrim- mages being fought out, and finally the ball rolled dead. Heathside were having all the play, and the ball becoming loose, Dale dribbled over the Benmont line and scored the first try for Heathside, Harter missing the goal.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Heathside in the Ascendant.

On resuming the Beamont forwards went away with a rush, but Griffiths drove them back with-a good touch kick. The ball came out to the Heath- side backs, and after a passing bout Taylor ran in under the sticks, for Dale to •add the extra points. Following the restart, the Beamont threequarter backs started a, passing movement, but Wych missed the final pass in attempting to double in. A minute later a movement by the Heathside backs was checked in fine style by Whitfield. 'Heathside pressed hard, and Forster, receiving, ran well and scored what seemed to be a good trY, but the touch- judge ruled him "in touch." The Heathens, how- ever, would not be denied, and just before the in- terval Dolphin scored, but Dale failed to convert. Half-time: Heathside, one , goal three tries (11 points); Beamont, nil. It was not long before Heathside opened their second half account, for from another passing move- ment Dale forced himself over, but Harter failed to convert. Shortly afterwards Harter was served when in a good position, and, beating the full-back, scored the fifth try of the afternoon. but Gleave missed the goal. Shortly before the finish Harter again scored. Griffiths adding the extra points. Result: Heathside, two goals six tries (22 points); Beamont, nil.

TO-DAY'S MATCH. Parochial v. Bcamont, on Bylancls's ground Kick-off 3.30. • Referee, Mr. G-. Crozier.

ANGLING.

GLADSTONE ARMS CONTEST.

The anpilal contest from headquarters attracted an entry of 40. The match took place at Newbridge Salt Works end. The officials were on the job early, as they travelled to the scene by taxi, leaving noth- ing to chance, and they had everything in apple- pie order when the members arrived. The secre- tary soon had all at their numbers, and although the morning was not one of the pleasantest it did not damp the ardoiir of the members, for-,no sooner had " time " been called, than " landed " was also called in several places. There never was a lull during the contest, either one or another was calling out di.tring the whole two hours. This match is the record catch from Warrington this year, the lowest weight to receive a prize being 14oz. 13dr. It was generally thought during the match that it would be necessary to have over 11b. to get in the prizes. The weight caught was 351 b. 14oz. 10dr.• 37 mem- bers weighed in; the other two who took part in the match returned their fish to the' water before the weighers reached them, seeing they had no chance. The winner was again found in our old champion, F. Jolley. [Mr. Printer, this is the third pencil I have worn but writing "F. Jolley, Ist."] He received a beautiful oak clock and bronze horses, presented to the society by our patron, S. Donoghue, along with the " Mather " challenge cup, this being the sixth occasion he has held the Cup. Up to the present we cannot think of a member who can hold his own with him in the River Weaver, his weight being 21b. 7oz. Bdr. Other winners: 2, R. Dunbobbin, 21b. 9dr.; 3, E. 11b. 12oz. Bdr.; 4, F. Trigger, 11b. 10oz. 4dr.; 5, J. Green. 11b. Boz. Bdr.; 6 J. Preston, 11b. 7oz. sdr.; 7, T. Perry [first year's novice: Well done!], 11b. 50z.; 8, G. Hamblett. 11b. 3oz. 4dr.; 9, R. Cook, 11b. 30z.; 10, A. Swarbrick, 11b. 2oz. Bdr.; and so on down to 14oz. 13dr. What a splendid contest!

THE BAY HORSE MATCH.

The contest from the above club WdS held at Norton on Saturday last, and drew record entry, 45 competing out of a possible 50. Although you cannot compare this water with the Waver for match' fishing, it always seems to draw a good entry. The officials of the Bay Horse were of the opinion that they would have good sport, as this length has been fishing up to the mark this last week or so. But they were unfortunate in catching it on or.c of its off days. The following day fix members filhed for two hours and took 101 b. of fish (what luck!), and on the Monday following A. Racey weighed in a roach Boz. 12dr. However, the officials can congratulate themselves on receiving such an entry. The winner was found in E. Hill, who seems to have taken the place of his churn (H. Moore, whit) is now on active service), as this is his second first in the canal this year; weight, 6oz. 13dr.; 2, J. Higham, 6 oz. 11dr. (and special fox largest fish); 3, P. Jolley, soz. 4dr. (and special for smallest fish) (Hill and Jolley tie for special for !nest fish); 4, E. Brookes, 40z sdr.; 5 and 6, W. Hewitt and G. Lilly, 3óz. 9dr.; 7, if. Brookes. 3oz. 4dr. • 8, T. Dixon, 30z.; 9, T. Roberts, 2oz. 11dr.; -10, W. Bailey, 2.0 z. 9dr.; and so on down to. loz. sdr.

At the prize distribution the members, who ha'd invited the treasurer of the Warrington N.A., Mr. E. Brookes, to fish in their match before joining up in His Majesty's forces, took the opportunity of presenting him with a wrist watch, Mr. T. Ttoberts, the treasurer of, the Bay 'Horse, strapping it on his wrist and wishing him " God-speed " and the best luck.

At this meeting it was decided to hold another contest at Norton in aid of members who are 'serv- ing in the Army, to provide them with comforts and things that are necessary for them, the first meeting to be, held at the Gladstone Arms on Monday next at 7.30 pm. ASSOCIATION. (Lancashire Combination.)

THE USUAL GOAL.

Town Go Down To South Liverpool In Stern Struggle.

That only one goal hould be scored whenever Warrington Town and South Liverpool meet seems to be an edict of the Gods. On two Occa- sions before Saturday the South have managed to pull off a victory with the only goal of the match, and now it has happened again., The teams are to match themselves -against each other at the Dingle to-day, too, and it will be particularly interesting to know the result. There are some superstitious pedple, of course, but there are others, and Warrington goes to Liverpool full of confidence, lacking nothing in motaie and energy.

Than the game at the Dingle last week I do not recollect a more stern .one. It was a struggle from start to finish and the 2,000 spectators had a sporting treat. Naturally, I was disappointed with the result, but it regis- tered the merits of the play. South Liverpool are a team to conjure with; they go for goal for all they are worth. They are not interested in the dainties of football, although, on occa- sions, they gave us episodes of the picturesque with their expert passing. From the kick-off— the ball was started by AsPinall—,it was obvious that a battle-royal was in the offing and War- rington engaged .with skill and determination.

An unfortunate circumstance was the un- avoidable and unexpected absence of George Whitfield, causing Fahey to be played at centre-half. With George as the pivot, I think there 'would have been nioie openings. niade ; as it was, however, in the first half the attacks were of equal number, and pretty frequent, too. Glover, at right-half, though, was rather weak —too unsteady and erratic—yet one must not forget that on two or three momentous occa- sions, particularly in the second half, he showed ingenuity in stopping' attacks, and whisked the ball up the field soundly. Owen and Gregson also were generally ineffective. All are players of finosse, but against the South such tactics are hopeless. Opposed to them were-. stolid stalwarts and they want some handling. Owen got badly shaken on two or three occasions, and, I think, a little nervousness was engendered. .

. That was the dominant weakness of the team. • Never at any .time, perhaps, were the forwards' brilliant, but, on the other hand, no palpable inefficiency brought tears. It was a fairly good line, even with Owen in his wrecked condition. The backs were splendid, and Kite was wonderful—the admired of the crowd, "a real hero he." And it was rather fortunate that Kite was astride his usual form. Very seldom when _South Liverpool shot was their aim far .out; often it was deadly accurate, and only the best of "goalies" could have defended their charges so well. He even saved a penalty shot, after about twenty minutes from the kick-off.' It was after Dawson and Parr had worked nicely Up the left, wing and Fahey had tripped the former in the penalty area. Bennett was entrusted with the free-kick, and, though shooting straight and fast, Kite's hands snatched the ball as in a vice, and, with .a bounce, it was away overhead. A director of South Liverpool told me that in his time Bennett has taken 31 penalty kicks, and this was the first one he has missed. Seven min- utes from time, when the South did score, Kite was in no wise to blame. By excellent com- bination. the . home -forwards had' got almost. within a stride—certainly within a hop, skip and a jump-P--of th 6 goalimmith, and a back failing not ingloricrusly—l think it was. Leak .-,the ball 'literally .ciiinbed' up , his figure,- over. his head, and, striking the. top of the net— the spectators yelled furiously at their victory. Though, I fancy, there was even then a sub-conscious feeling of sorrow that Kite had been beaten. for' they had an idep that Kite was about the best goalkeeper that was ever inade!

There were yet seven minutes to go. For a considerable time Liverpool had been attacking sternly and skilfully—an offensive en one wing having failed, they would try the other, and then down the centre. It was thrilling to watch. Never were backs more sorely tried, and nearly tbe whole of the Warrington men fen back to prevent their comrades' immersion. But it was the • adoption of this defensive method which culminated in the missing of an equalising goal. Ditchfield had sneaked th ball in the course of its passage to the centrb and forthwith winged it to Siitdall, who, ;void- ing Smith, slung it over to Owen, and he, after a swift dribble forward slipped the ball well into centre and there were only a back and the goalkeeper for the five to beat. Unfortunately. that "five" were only four, however, for Aspinall, intent on defending, and, presumably none too hopeful of the outburst of the left wink, had not yet come up to the line, tind Jenkinson pounced on the ball and relieved the situation for the South in splendid style.

For vigour South Liverpool cannot• be ex- celled in the League. They are not without subtlety either. In the latter half, when War- rington were facing a strong sun, Liverpool ballooned the ball continually in their attacks, and, our fellows, looking up, had difficulty in locating its flight. Liverpool knew what they were doing..

As stated already, the game was intensely in- teresting; every one admitted it to be an excel- lent exposition of the game by botlk teams; and that a very large crowd will be at the Dingle to-day may be anticipated with certainty. The possibility of seeing another game like that. on Saturday last is not .to be missed without good reason.

The teams were

Warrington: 'Kite; Leah and Millward; Glover, Fahey and Ditehfield; Gregson, SiddalL Aspinall, Giblin and Owen. South Liverpool: Moore; Weldin and Jenkin- son; Smith, Ted Williams and Owen Williams; Hall, Hannaway, Bennett, Dawson and Parr.

A strong team is going to South Liverpool to-day on the 1.50 from the Central Station. The players to be selected from are: Kite; Leah, Cookson and Milward; Heywood (Manchester United), Whitfield and Bullen; Aspinall, Siddall, Rubotham, Giblin and Owen.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT JUNIOR LEAGUE.

An the clubs in the above competition were engaged last week, and it is very evident froin the heavy scoring in some of the games that some of the Juniors have not been long in get- ting into their stride. No fewer 'than 27 goals were sco'red during the afternoon. The winning teams claim 23 send the losers had only four in their favour.

The St.' Elphin's played their first match on their new ground, and had Higbeebl Juniors as visitors. At the' close of play they were returned easy winners byl seven goals against one.

The Bewsey Adult School team Visited Victoria Park for the second week in .succession, but on this occasion they were in opposition to the Cadets. As both these teams were defeated the previous week a keen game was afiticipated, but the Bewsoy lads had come with a determination to wipe out the defeat of their previous visit, and were successful in scoring four )times in each half. The Cadets Must have been non. p/ussed by the performanT of their opponnts, as they were. only able to respond on one occasion. On the whole the display of the Cadets was disappointing. For the visitors Bradburn was in fine shooting form, claiming four of the goals, and Roylance (2), Fear and Schofield completed the total.

A Niery hard game was witnessed between the Recs. and Orford Juniors, the latter showing finer combination and were better target shooters, scored on four occasions, whilst the Recs. only replied once. Lockhart, despite the score against him, saved many fine shots, and

he received good assistance from Willcocks it back. The Juniors gave a. more evenly balanced nisplay and Witton was at his best at centre- half. Fitzgerald, Hurst and Belshaw were prominent in the front line, and Doughty was good in defence.

The Penketh Juniors surpassed their own •supporters ip their opening home fixture against Howley -United, and sustained defeat by four goals to one. This reverse was very dis- appointing to the home supporters, as the pre- vious week in their opening fixture on foreign soil they gained a very easy vietory over the Highfield Juniors. The meeting: with Howley was looked forvard to very confidently. There- fore the reverse was a great surprise to all con- nected with the club. Every credit must be given to the Howley lads for their victory, which was well-earned, as they were easily the better team on the day's play, and the honours of victory might be equally shared by all the team. Pearson, Glover, Thomasson and Moss were the yick of the home team.

During the progress of the match a very re- grettable accident happened to Watson, of the home team. It was during an attack by the visitors that he fell to the ground with his arm 'under him, and it was found that he had sus- tained a broken wrist. Everybody was very sympathetic with the unfortunate player, and wish him a speedy recovery.

Owing to the. war, -which has caused a great amount of uncertainty of being able to get sufficient players to form a team, the Emmanuel and the Warrington Central Clubs, who have been accustomed to play in the Vi Aoria lark for several seasons, and were only able to . get a team together as the football season com- menced, were; owing to the circumstances, com- pelled to delay making their application to the Parks Committee for permission to play in Victoria Park again. They have received re- plies that their applications are too late, as the grounds have already - been allotted to the Juniors. As the playing mentbers of these teams are engaged on munition work their applications should be reconsidered by the Parks Committee - and, additional.. space be. granted-. to .them, as there is one point which is greatly in the clubs' favour; that accommodation is .made for four Rugby grounds and only tw.3 for the Association . game, and amongst the juniors the Association game is more keenly followed than the Rugby game.

PENKETH JUNIORS v. HOWLEY UNITED.

On the Recreation ground. Penketh Juniors: J. Moss; Newall, Thomasson; White, Watson, Boyle; Magee, Glover, Pearson, Ainsworth, Finch. Howley United: Walters; Rowlinson, Foster; Atherton, Miller, Hind; Boyle, Rylance, Davies, Woodhouse, Humphries.

Referee, Mr. P. Gosling. The visitors won the toss and played with the wind at their backs, and also the slope in their favour. Pearson set the ball rolling, and at once advanced to the visitors' goal, where the effort was spoiled by Finch being in an off-side position. From the ,free kick the visitors were enabled to make an assault on the home goal, where Newall and Thomasson defended finely. Some keen play was witnessed. and Miller, for the visitors, checked many attempts of the home forwards, and play was transferred to the home goal again, when a corner kick was conceded. This was well placed, and some lively exchanges took place in the home goalmouth, Davies finally hooking the ball between the uprights and opening the score for the`visitors. Encouraged by this success the visitors again attacked the home citadel very hotly and increased their lead. A few minutes later slack play by the home defenders allowed Boyle to get through and put the visitors further ahead. Halftime: Howley United. 3 goals; Penlath Juniors, nil. —The- homesters were quickly in , evidence in the second half, and .after several efforts had been re- pulsed by the visitors' backs, Pearson made a splendid individual effort and scored the best goal of the match for the home team. From the centre kick the visitors became aggressive, and the home backs were severely tested. A penalty kick was given for hands, and Woodhouse failed to add the points. The home team were handicapjPed by losing the services of Watson for the remainder of the game, the latter unfortunately breaking his wrist. The visitors now had the best of the exchanges, and Moss was cheered by the spectators for his fine goal- keeping. The home goal was captured again before the call of time. Result: 'Howley United, 4 goals; Penketh Juniors, 1 goal.

LATCHFORD CADETS v. BEWSEY A.S.

In the Victoria Park, before a fair crowd of spectators.

Cackets: Oreenhalgh; Williams, Jones; Porter, Massey, Cook; Cartwright, Ashton, Smith, Mayors, Gibson.

Bewsey A.S.: 'Perks; 'Main, Stephens; Worral, Kent, Dickenson.; Hassell, Roylance, Bradburn, Fear, Schofield. Refe.ree, Mr. R. Williams.

The opening exchanges were keenly tontested, and each goal NVas visited without any success. After ten minutes' play the Bewsey forwards combined well together, and Bradburn beat Greenhalgh with a good shot. The visitors were quickly on the aggressive again, but good play by Williams and Jones relieved the situation. Then followed ,a brief 'visit to the visitors' goal, where Main and Stephens proved too good for the home forwards. Bradburn again beat Greenhalgh, and this was followed with a third from Roylance. From the centre kick the Cadets made a strong rally, but Perks defied their efforts, and they had to retreat in defence of their' own goal. where Royla.nce wait successful again. Half-time: Bewsey A.S., 4 goals; Cadets, 1 goal. The pecond half opened in favour of the Cadets, and several determined efforts were repulsed by the visitors' defence, and following a spell of midfield play. Gibson broke away on the right and swung the ball over to Cartwright. The latter centred finely. for Smith to put the ball past Perks. After this the Cadets had to play on the defensive, and in suite of their efforts further goals were stored by Bradburn, Hassell. Fear and Schofield. Result: Bewsey A.S., 8 g,oahr; Latchford Cadets, 1 goal.

LATCHFORD RECS. V. ORFORD JUNIORS.

In Victoria, Park

Latchford Recs.: G. Lockhart; Cooper, Willcocks; Trainor, Guest, Fairbrother; James, Raymond, Green, Hesson, Dutton.

lOrford Juniors: J. Walters; Griffiths, Doughty;_ Jones, Witton, Austin,;„ Belsha.w, Clare, Hurst, Fitzgerald, Roughley.

Referee Mr. Burgess _ _

The Recs. at once visited the visitors' goal area, where Doughty relieved with a big kick, which en- abled the Juniors to transfer play to the home goal. Lockhart had to save shots in quick succession from Belshaw, Hurst and Fitzgerald. Even 'play was followed by another attack by the Juniors, when Austin opened their score with a hard drive. The Juniors were having the best of the game, and from a good centre by Fitzgerald. Witton obtained second goal. The Recs. adopted kick-and-rush tac- tics, and Green tested Walters with a long shot, but the latter proved very safe. A little later the game was being contested in the Recs.' half, and Lockhart, coming out of goal to meet a long shot, missed the ball, and Hurst had not much Oifficultv in scoring. Half-time: Orford, 3 goals; Recs., nil. Resuming, the Recs, played in determined fashion and were soon pegging away at the visitors' goal. Willcocks hitting the upright with a fast shot, but directly, after Green made amends by scoring for the Recs., The Orford lads combined well, and from:a good centre by Belshaw, Hurst was only a few inches wide with a low shot. Fitzgerald was playing well for the Juniors, and making a neat pass to Roughley, he enabled the latter to beat the home- backs and score the visitors' fourth goal. After midfield play Walters had to save a good shot from Jaines, and at t,'‘e opposite end Cooper intercepted Belshaw, when in a dangerous position. A well-placed. corner kick by Roughley was placed over the bar by -Witton. Result: Orford Juniors. 1 goals; Latchford Recs., 1 goal.

Churches Express Gratitude In Prayer And Song.

Thanks for the good harvest has been the theme of the services at several Warrington and district churches during the week and at the week-end.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
BEWSEY ROAD WESLEYAN.

On Sunday the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church held their harvest festival, and there was an abundance of gifts all tastefully arranged. In the morning the preacher was the Rev. A. J. Morton and in the evening the Pastor the Rev. W. T. Derry). The hymns sung were all appropriate, as also wer% the prayers offered. PADGATE WESLEYAN CHURCH.

The harvest festival services in connection with the Padgate Wesleyan Church were held on Sunday, the preacher in the morn- ing being Mr. J. Upson, of Warrington. Speak- ing from Hebrews NH., 11, he said that we are not to test or judge God's purposes by the process, but by the complete results. Why have not all the prayers been answered to end this great conflict? God is producing and perfect- tag all that is best in us.. We must have greater trust and confidence in God and learn the great lesson that is taught us, by the har,7 vest.

In the afternoon a children's service was held, conducted by the Rev. A. J. Morton. His chief thought was thankfulness to all who at any time did anything to help us; thanks to parents and teachers for kindnesses showri and thank- fulness to God for the .many benefits which we enjoy.

th-e Rev. A. J. Morton also conducted the evening service, and speaking from Genesis viii., 22, he pointed out that gold is all right for its purchasing powers, but if there is no corn to purchase it is useless. He also emphasised the unspeakable worth of thA farmer. The anthems "Angel voices ever singing " (E. Vine Hall) and "Praise the Lord, ye heavens adore Him" (Edmund Turner), were sung by the choir, Mr. W. H. Ward officiating at the organ. The church had been very taste- fully decorated for the occasion with fruits and flowers, which were afterwards distributed amongst the wounded soldiers at the Barracks and Whitecross Hospitals.

_ CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTOR.

Handed Over To The Military At Stockton Heath.

Yesterday, before .a Special Court at Stockton Heath, composed of Messrs. James Hepherd and George Dakin, a young man, about 25 years of age, named Stanley Hillsborough Bodle, of Oaklands, Hunt's-lane, Latchford Without, was charged in custody with having failed to report. • Defendant Pleaded "Not Guilty."

Police-Constable Kelford stated that on the 30th May he received a notice from the Army authorities saying that the prisoner had been absent without leave since the 23rd May. He went to his home and saw his Mother, who said that he had left -home: . She showed witness a letter written by the prisoner, in which he stated that he was leaving home in ,order to avoid military Service and that he, would keep away until the Government recognised the conscientious objector. The previous night -witness met defendant in Hunt's-lane and asked him if he had anything to show. Why he was not in the Army. He replied: "No; r suppose you know I am a constientious objector and as such cannot recognise the military rultis." 'He admitted having received two notices to join Uri. Witness arrested him and brought him to the lock-up. Defendant said that what the witness had stated was, quite correct. Captain Luck, Chester Castle, gave, evidence -as to the service of notice calling defendant up. Defendant returned the notice with a letter -in the terms referred to by previous 'Witness. Sergeant Wilding said the prisoner had given the authorities a great deal of trotkble. Defendant said that according to the law he should be handed over to the. civil authorities. The Chairman said he was in the hands of the military authority at the present ,moment. Defendant said he ;could not take up Military duties, as it was against the -principles in which he had been brought up. The Chairman asked where he had been brought up. Defendant mentioned the International Bible Students' Association, who say that the whole teaching of the Bible' is onoc.se(no _war.

TheChOrnian: You will Jive to do your duty like everyone eNe.

I)efenl'ant: I am doing my duty, I -- The Chaiiman: You are not. You .should join the Colours. Defendant was then fined 40s. and handed Over to the military authorities.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
Unique Concert En Parr Hall Last Night.

EXQUISITE PERFORMANCE

Our greatest orator could not make a more striking appeal on behalf of our blinded heroes than the concert given in the Parr Hall last evening by a number of blind musicians. The occasion was unique, bearing in mind the object of the entertainment, and it is little won- der that there were few vacant seats: The blind pleaded for the blind, through the irresistible medium of Music, and what heart could remain unmoved by such .powerful eloquence? The National Institute for tbe Blind sent forth of ith best to‘ arouse and solidify sympathy on behalf of the stricken hi- mates of St. Dunstan's Hostel for our blinded soldiers and ailors'—men from whom the visual ,delights of this world have been blotted out for ever.

c- A pathetic note was struck is each per- former was gently led on the platform in 'front of the audience; but the tragedy of our blinded warriors was only half displayed. These musi- cians live in another world than ours. Some- one has said, "The brightest star shines in the darkest hour "—the star of hope. St. Dunstan's Hostel will open up new ambitions to those whose existence has suddenly become ,a blank. It is comparatively easy to-understand that :the Braille system. has developed musicians, but the natural gifts must be present, and this fact was demonstrated by our friends last evening. We had a blind composer, Mr. W. Wolstenholme, Mus.Bac. (Oxon), who delighted the audience with some peculiarly characteristic pianoforte solos. They were instructive as being wholly suggestive of sound. There was no sight colour; Witness the descriptive morceau, "The Tor- rent." His attitude at the instrument, his delicacy, precision and accuracy of touch were extraordinary when one remembered that the performer was blind. Most praiseworthy also were the violin selec- tions by Mr. Ernest Whitfield, who, although he walked on to the stage by himself, is almost blind and only able to distinguish between light and darkness. The violin, however, might naturally be considered the readiest and corn- pletest interpreter of a blind musician's soul. It was amply proved by his rendering of Saint- Saens's pretty composition, "Introduction and Rondo," with double stopping, and Sarasate's lively "Gipsy Melodies:" Of the vocalists there 'is nothing to say but praise. • Whether as soloists or in concert they sang most effectively. Miss Ada Jackson (soprano) .was loudly encored for her exquisite rendering •bf "_The Swallows" (Coen), and Mr. Angus Brown, who contributed "111 'sing thee songs of Araby" in faultless style, gave "Annie 'Laurie" as a r,e-call. Mr. E. E. W. Littlewood (baritone) and Miss Ada Thornewell (contralto) were no less •successful in their selections and were the recipients of well- merited applause. The concluding quartet, "Our Empire in Arms" (arranged by Collard), was a fine-spirited vocalistic.effort. The accompanist was Miss Ethel Bartlett, and Mr. Avalon Collard ably direoted•the\concert. During the interval Mr. Avalon Collard gave a short interesting address on the work of Our Blinded Soldiers and Sailors at St. Dunstan's Hostel, illustrated by lantern slides.

Tke artistes and audience were thanked by the Mayor (Alderman Peacock) and Sir Arthur Crosfield, ;Bart., the latter remarking, in addition, that he and Lady Crosfield had known Sir Arthur and Lady Pearson for many years, and had taken a deep interest in the work the eminent blind Baronet had carried on at St. Dunstan's, and it was at his suggestion to him (the speaker) that that con- cert was held. They had certainly had a great musical treat, and he hoped they would have similar one again. (Applause.) In coming that evening they had certainly been supporting one of the best institutions in the country. (Ap- plause.)

A PILGRIMAGE OF THE ENIPIRE.

By Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart. London: Eyre and Spottiswode, Ltd. 2s. 6d. net; cloth, is. This is a call to arms to England and to the Empire. "I have endeavoured," says the author in a very unnecessary apologia, "to convey some sense of the magnitude of all we are striving for"; and he has succeeded. Not one of the 139 stanzas but abounds with patriotism of peculiar dignity—even austerity. After the brief emotional passage with which the poem opens, the, appeal is more to the reason than to the impulse; and there is con- sequently disclosed a remarkable breadth of out- look in all branches of history and art. There are no bypath 4 to be wandered; the theme throughout is adhered to with an almost ruth- less concentration. Indeed, brevity would appear to have_been the illoet's one desire: "If, reader, it would seem that _we have tarried," he interjects after a passage to which one is tempted to apply his own phrase: Strains of a colour symphony sublime.

• .) The Spensermn metre is, of course, one beset with pitfalls: yet in this respect there are few slips in the "Pilgrimage," the scansion being consistently simple except in Stanza 19, where spondee and iambus alternate with e quaint effect. On the other hand there occur passages of considerable worth:

No palace, temple, amphitheatre, dome On which the toiling multitudes have spent Vast labour In grandeur and in dignity compare With Kar Nak : here 'tis poetry hath lent Repose and pathos; come in the night air. Reader, to view a scene so eloquent and fair. The plea for Greece's Alliance is extremely powerful, for here the poet is in his element: Greece and her destiny were hid by night: Lo! Venizelos spake and all was light.

In statesmanship and courage his career With loftiest traditions cloth' invite Noble comparison; a duty clear He demonstrtites for those who hold their honour dear.

" The Pilgrimage of the Empire," which is dedicated to all "who in the great war are, or have been, 'fighting the battle of the Empire." is, beautifully illustrated with a series of rare views in. British dominions and in the Mother Country itself, printed upon special leaves. The binding is admirably executed in dark cloth with gdld lettering. "A Pilgrimage of the Empire" is obtainable at all booksellers and iailway bookstalls. The entire profits arising from the sale of the book will be given to Sir Arthur Pearson's fund for those who have lost their eyesight in the war.'

PENKETH.

Village Soldier on Wondehs of India.—Private Leonard Smith, son of Mr. James and Mrs. Smith, Victoria-road, writing home, tells ,of the wonderful scenery he has lately been marching through in the Mysore district of India. The tropical vegetation, . so utterly opposite to our Lancashire flora, has 'held him spellbound . with its vastness and beauty. As his battalion were the first representatives of the British Army that most of the natives had ever -seen, their curiosity and excitement can be easily imagined. On one or two occasions they. had to dig down through the sand to obtain water for much-needed ablutions, but the liquid when reached proved .to be opaque and exceedingly dirty:- The one great excite- ment which will ever live in ,his memory was when His Highness Maharaja of Mysore sent an invitation to the whole of the battalion to be his -guest S for a day. The usual review was held, followed by the 'march past in various formations. When these military' functions were over the Maharaja's palace,- *ith its un- dreamed of brillianee; was thrown open to offi- cers and men for inspection. The magnificent building suddenly burst into a blaze of glorious light; some 15,0Q0 electric globes shone forth. With feelings of awe the party inspected gal- leries of choice. marble, ceilings beautifully moulded, delicately tinted, and gilded with per- fect workmanship; colours so perfectly blended and so softly shaded (seemed above the work of than); saw doors of sandalwood and silver, in- comparably carved and - chased; and went through the throne room into a state room, which held every eye spellbound with sheer delight; not forgetting a cosy music' room, re- plete with a grand piano . and a magnificent organ. Small wonder they came out of the palace amazed and excited, which ended in one and all giving vent to their feelings in the good old British way.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMAN corroN SPINNER

Sues Employers Unsuccessfully At County Court.

On Thursday, in the County Court, his Honour Judge Scott Fox heard an action in which Mrs. Beatrice Galvin, of 47, Eldon- street, Warrington, cotton spinner, sought for' compensation from • Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, Cockhedgo Mills, in respcct of injuries sustained by her pn the 26th May. The re- spondents denied liability on the ground that the injuries were not received in !Le course of applicant's employment. Mr. Spafford, barrigter (instructeil by Mr. C. H. Unsworth, Warrington), appeared for applicant, and Mr. Wingate Saul, barrister (in- structed by Mr. John Taylor, Manchester), represented the respondents. Mr. Spafford said that applicant, a married woman, had ,been employed by respondents, and was a cotton spinner. She was what was called "three-sider," meaning that she worked two sides of one loom and one side of another loom. Her wages were 12s. 4d. a week. Amongst her duties was that of keeping her machine clean. For that purpose an instrument was providett, which was passed along the frame. Over the cog-wheel at the end of the frame there was an iron cover, which could be raised. This system of cleaning by " wipers " had been only lately introduced , into the mill. Having done the wiping, it was necessary to raise this cover to take out the brush. About 12 o'clock on the 26th May applicant was cleaning the frame, and when she lifted the box with her left hand she saw a bobbin which had got to the cog-wheel. In trying to get at the bobbin her, right hand slipped on the cog-whels and the ends of the second and third fingers were caught. She went to the Infirmary and it was found neces- sary to amputate two of her fingers—the tip of one finger and the top of the other down to the second joint. She must have been a very courageous young woman because; an hour after the operation she came down to the mill awl saw, Mr. Ainswiorth, the manager, and Jim Hindes, the foreman. . - .

In reply to his Honour, Counsel said that applicant had not gone back to work and the Judge said that in that case the claim would be for half wages, 6s. 2d., until further order. Applicant, who is 23 years of age, stated that she had been a spinner since she was fifteen. It was the minder's business to clean the machine. She had always been told • to keep necessary things out of the cog-wheels which happened to fall in. Mr. Spafford : What do you mean by neces- sary things ?—Anything that might cauN,,, damage. If the bobbin had 'gone into the cog-wheel what would have 'happened P—There would be a breakdown and I would have had to make an explanation.

===== 0002601_19160923_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
Applicant Cross-examined.

Applicant said that on going back to the mill she saw Mr. Ainsworth, the manager, and he said she had no right to lift the cover up. She replied that Jim Hindes was always complain- ing to her about lost things being in the wheels, and Mr. Ainsworth said "Don't mind blaming it on Jim Hindes."

In cross-examination by Mr. Saul, applicant said the biabbin was under the axle and not under the wheel. While, as a matter of fact, it was not necessary to lift the cover to get the wiper out, because it could be done by break- ing the last thread, they did not break the last thread. They were shown how to lift the covers by Mr. Hindes: the cover was sometimes lifted up if the wheels were squeaking, and the over- looker would be sent for.

Mrs. Rutter, who had worled at the mill for about 12 years, also gave evidence. They were always told to keep the covers clear of any articles. If a bobbin chanced to get under the cover she would have taken it out; otherwise it would have cauSed damage to the frame. There was no notice posted up about the covers. Mr. Saul: Was it your ,business to fetch Hindes if you heard squeaking F—We should find out where the squeaking was first.

What the Overlooker Said.

The only witness called for the respondents was Mr. James Hinde, who had been in the position of overlooker in the mills for about 15 years. These wipers were not in use when applicant came into the employment. Those who started in the mill were told with regard to this particular thing that on no occasion were they to lift these covers to any extent any higher than it was necessary to get the wiper out. If the machine squeaked it was not their place to lift the cover, their duty was to stop the machine, because they had no idea what it was that was wrong. It would be a breach of duties, even if there was squeaking,. to lift up the cover. In all his experience he never knew of a bobbin getting under the cover. The only thing be knew of getting under the cover was a cleaning cloth, and then they should stop the frame to take it Out. By Mr. Spafford : It was left to the discretion of the girls whether they stopped the frame or reported it to him. He had never actually seen any girl open a gear box. It Was their duty to keep the boxes free, by senceng down the cleaning cloths. Mr. Saul (for the respondents) said that the box guard in question was put there as required by the Factory and Workshops' Act, by which employers are obliged to fence off securely any dangerous part of machinery. Counsel sub- mitted that the accident did not arise out of 4.r in the course of the applicant's employment. He asked his Honour to say that Mrs. Galvin knew that she had no right to lift this cover. and that it was her duty to stop the machine and report the matter._ .

his Honour said that he should come to the conclusion that his award must be for the respondents. There was a ,concensus of evidence that speaking generally,. these women were required not to touch this box at all. In; his opinion applicant had no obligation or excuse in regard to her duty to her employer upon such an occasion, or for such a reason to do what was against the whole rule and practice of the establishment and the whole purpose of the machinery, to insert her fingers into the jaws of this dangerous place. In his opinion the applicant's case failed. His award was. therefore, for the respondents. Mr. Saul said his clients did not ask for costs.

STAGE AND KINEMA.

Royal Court Theatre. In these latter months, since Mr. Harry War- burton has been the manager, we have had a succession of interesting productions, well varied. .As a domestic drama, "The Little Grey- Home in the Wt," which is being staged this week by Mr. and Mrs. Ernest Vatighan's com- pany, has had few more successful rivals.

I saw it on the night it was first produced

at the theatre of the authoress, Mrs. F. G. Kimberley, and had the pleasure of writing a column notice of it for "The Stage." It was, therefore, as a renewal of old acquaintanceship that I met Mr. Vaughan the evening. For four years Mr. Vaughan and his wife, Miss Madge Douglas, have been touring Mrs.- Kim- berley's plays. They were here someotime ago in the "Prairie Outhyw," and the good 'impres- sion they gave then is maintained in "The Little Grey .Home in the West." Miss '.Douglas is a player of personality, and it was to suit her individuality, particularly, that Mrs. ,Kim- berley wrote the one now in possession. at the Royal Court. Dainty, demure and spirited, she makes ,a charming and fascinating Peggy,. •

•, The company itself,-as well as the scenery, is of excellent quality. Mr. Vaughan himself knows how to act and run a play. For a long time he was with thg Irish players. Seven or eight years ago he wits manager of the famous Abbey Theatre, Dublin. As for his speech, if he wanted to convince anyone that he wasn't Irish, he would have to do it in writing. has the brogue beautifully.

I have pleasure in announcing that Mr. John Foster Fraser, the brilliant journalist and lecturer, is coming to give. two war talks on a Sunday afternoon and evening in October. Most people know the raciness and knowledge with which he invests his writings, and, having heard him lecture on several occasions, I may say that they will find .him as interesting to listen to as he is to read.

Next week and hereafter twmatifiees a week will be given at the Royal Court—on Mondays and Thursdays. A better start off in the quality of plays and players need not be desired than Mr. Raynor and his repertoire. The plays he intends submitting are: Monday ,matinee and Saturday night, "A Bunch of Violets "; Mon- day and Wednesday nights,' 'mile Dancing Girl "; Tuesday and Thursday afternoon and evening, "Moths"; and on Friday , night, "Lady Huntworth's Experiment."

Palace and Hippodrome.

The Hippodrome programmes have one special feature. They do not come within the strictures which we have recently read in the Pres. The turns are bright and inoffensive. Prominent this week is the turn of 'oden Williams, who .entertains with song and story. His songs and patriotic pieces are all original, while his problem 'play skit is a neat bit of work. He is clever and refined as well as original.

Mr. Williams has many happy memories of Warrington, where he has quite a number of friends'. lie was intimately connected with the, town ,not many years ago, when he acted as professional coach for the Operatic Society., Under his able directorship the society per- formed a number of operas with signal success, notably "The Pirates of Penzance" and "The Yeomen of the Guard."

In common with some of the best of Our platform entertainers he has delighted numer- ous audiences and followed their lead some years ago by appearing on the music-hall stage. ,He has been fortunate in so doing, as he has found his houses most appreciative and his experience up to the present has been one of continued success. He has been engaged for the whole, of the Macnaghten tour. Mr. Wil- liams has happy recollections of being person- ally congratulated by the late Sir Henry Irving, before whom he recited at the Manchester Arts Club.

Fred Mottle and Co. present a comedy sketch, "Poor Old George," which shows how a run- away proposition is frustrated. Gene Morelle, the comedy tramp musician, with his stage full of odds and ends, is very laughable; and Victor and Louis, golfer and Caddie, create much fun. Other good turns are :—L. Martinek, in orginal Lady Rag Doll act; the Seven Dandy Lassies, a troupe of clever children; Loie Conn, surprise cornettist; and Phyllis Bray, vocalist.

The bill for next week includes Harry Russell, the Irishman from York Mare; Allan MacDonald, in' a Scottish sketch; the "Two B'Hoys," Allan Martin and Walter O'Brien; and Flora and Alberta, in a hovel speciality. "Twists and Twirls." Then we are promised Rosa Hamel, who, as a caricaturist, draws. with both hands; Richard Rawson, in dramatic and humorous studies, and Les Van DeUrens, musi- cal acrobats.

Music hall goers will be pleased to learn that commencing on October 2nd, there will be a full variety show each Monday afternoon at 2.45 o'clock, when the performances will be exactly the same as at the evening entertain- ments.

The Khtemas. • There is now showing at the Star Kinema House, Church-street, a thrilling drama, "The Flash, of an Emerald," featuring Robert War- wick. For the first half of next week we are to have a Special triangle photo play "Peggy," 4n which Billy Burke is the chief character; and for the latter half a fine drama entitled "Hazel Kirke" and part one of a great serial story of circus life, "Peg o' the Ring."

At the Grand Electric 'Theatre, Marguerite Snow. is now appearing as Peg in "Peggy of the Cotton ' Mills." During the first half of next

week "The Blindness of Love," starring Grace Valentine, and the seventeenth episode of "The Diamond from the Sky" will be screened; while for the latter half there will be shown a fine classical and humorous four-act picture entitled "Niobe," featuring Hazel Dawn. ,

At the Pavilion there is now being screened "Sir," a picture-ised version of the novel by Sir H. Rider Haggard. For the first half of next week a splendid Transatlantic drama, in which Violet Mersereau and William Garwood. take leading parts, will be shown, the title Sf the film being "A Fight for Fortune," and also the seventeenth episode of "The Diamond from the Sky." RAILWAY FARE ALLOWED.

PYRAMIDS OF FLOWERS.

Gorgeous Exhibition By Blind And Crippled Girls. i

•A wonderfully charming scene meets the elO as one . enters the Co-operative Hall, Sanker street, where a floral exhibition and sale of beautiful artificial flowers, made by blind and crippled girls, was started yesterday afternoon, and will continue until the 30th inst. The hall is a blaze of colour. Thousands of myriad coloured flowers grace lattice-work stalls all round the room and in the centre. 111111 platform is a picture, and from it strains o music, vocal and instrumental, are continik ously coming. Tea gardens give it a social 0:e well as horticultural complexion. Seated in various parts of the hall, making more flowers, are poor crippled girls. The opening ceremony took place yesterdaY afternoon, in the presence of a large gatherinf of friends. The affair has the support cm' churches of all denominations. Ladies fool -nearly every church and chapel in the town are giving their services as stall-holders. . Among those with the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) on the platform yesterday I%.ere Mrs. Rylands, Sir Arthur Croeld, Bit, the A. Pc-vs. Canons Willis and Stevnnson, and the Revs. B. Percival, W. T. Derry, T. T. Jan° and H. Ogden. In a short speech, the Mayor gave the exhibi tion a happy send-off, mentioning that 017 crippled girls had made all that wonderfn' array of flowers, and by purchasing their wares they were helping them to be self-,supportink The best help was self-help. Practical Self-Help. Mr. Ernest Trott, who represents the Jelin:, Groom's Crippleage and Flower Girls' Missin' (London), said the institution ranked as one of national importance. Besides accothmodat- ing crippled girls during the fifty years of it° - existence, the institution had been instrurann tal in rescuing from the dangers of street life three thousand girls and finding for them suit: able employment. There was a great deal force in the argument that life was not worth living for those that were afflicted, and the/ were doing all they could to make them hapPY. and useful by making them wage-earners int!: pleasant way. In a year, throughout 0%7" country, as much as two million pounds spent 'in the purchasing .of artificialflovver6i; and they had their "share in that amount. there were any crippled girls in Warrlllgtl who did not know what to do for a living, an they came ':within the rules of the institutioill they would be pleased. to give them a trial alle help them on the way to becoming wage- earners. Mrs. Rylands, who declared the exhibition open, thought they ought to do everything rs support that organisation. Many of the gird were from poor homes and Workhouses, en without that work they had no happy future' Sir Arthur Crosficld And The Sure path T° Happiness. Walking by the aid of a crutch, a criPPle4, girl then presented Mrs. Rylands with

beautiful bouquet. The pleasure of proposing a vote. of 011n3? to the Mayor and Mrs. RYlands was in the hanns of Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart. He also took dr opportunity of expressing the hope that the el': hibition would be a great and practical sueees.',: lie thought that one of the greatest factors 119 the work was that it helped to make the gi happier and more self-respecting, because, their occupation, they were able to accoi3ll9r what was both ust-ful and pleasant. And, alwo all, there wkis no surer path to happiness thd• the feeling that one was doing something 11,r,s ful and pleasant. (Hear, hear.) HOUSE ON FIRE LAST NIGIIT.

During the temporary absence at the Pall and Hippodrome of Mr. and Mrs. Crane, West-street, a fire broke out at their residerl about nine o'clock last ,night. The Fire gade was summoned, and, in the meanthe , buckets of water were effectively used ,by t, neighbours. PRACTICES WILL BE RESUMED, for Chorus and Orchestra, in CAIRO-STREET SCHOOLROOM on. TUESDAY, SEPTEMBER '26th, at 7.30 p.m. The Conductor will be in attendance at 6.50 to receive intending new members. In the present shortage of men, owing to the war, the Committee will welcome the services of all male singers, especially those who may have come into the town on munition work, and who have not yet attached themselves to any choral society or choir. Such vocalists may join the Musical Society's Chorus without the payment of any fees, the only expense they will be put to being the cost of music, _Which, for the first concert, will not exceed is. 6d. THE WI TER CAMPAIGN. ...I COMFORTS FOR OUR TROOPS AT THE -rßONT—France. Mesopotamia, Egypt, Salonica,-- and for our SOLDIERS IN HOSPITAL at home and abroad. The Warrington Branch of the National Com- ttee, comprising over 500 ladies, has already made and despatched through Sir Edward Ward, Director- Generaj. many thousands of articles. Materials for making and providing urgently- needed supplies to prevent Frostbite, Rheumatism, Chest Troubles and other evils, inevitable in trench warfare, must be secured at once. NEW MEMBERS. 1005 Violet Lowery, 39, Leicester-street; age 11; Evelyn-street School. • 1006 Amy Campbell, 56, Lancaster-street; age 13; Evelyn-street School. • 1007 Doris Whittaker, 62, Baxter-street; age 12; Evelyn-street School. 1008 Nancy Lehrle, 194, Battersby-lane; age 10; Beamont School. "GREATLY ADMIRED." Niece Dorothy Lowery, 39, Leicester-street, who won the prize last week, writes:— "Dear Uncle Jack,—l now take the pleasure of writing to thank you for the nice prize which I received last Saturday. It was greatly admired by my friends and teachers, who said I was very lucky to win it the first • time I wrote." THE GIRL WHO SMILES. The wind was east, and the chimney smoked, And the old brown house seemed dreary. For nobody smiled and nobody joked, The young folks grumbled, the old folks croaked, And they came home chilled and weary. Then opened the door and a girl came in; Oh, she was homely—very; Her face was plain, and her cheek was thin, There wasn't a dimple from brow to chin, But her smile was bright and cheery. She spoke not a word of the cold or the damp, Nor yet of the gloom about her, But she mended the fire, and lighted the lamp, And she put on the place a different stamp, From that it had without her. They forgot that the house was a dull old place. And smoky from base to rafter, And gloom departed from every face As they felt the charm of her mirthful grace And the cheer of her happy laughter. Oh, give me the girl who will smile and sing, And make all glad together! To be plain or fair is a lesser thing, But a kind, unselfish heart can bring Good cheer in the darkest weather.

second goal, and a little later Robotham missed scoring from a penalty kick. The visitors continued to have the best of the game, and Brinkswater• con- ceded a corner when he ought to have cleared easily. Robotham, by a piece of good individual Play, again lowered the home goal, and the interval arrived with the score: Brunswick Villa, 3 goals; Sankey Athletic, nil. The home team, with the aid of the wind, were quickly attacking the visitors' citadel, and a smartly hooked shot by Hunt was well got away by Law- rence.• Some even exchanges were seen in midfield, and then the home team led another attack on their opponents' goal. After Lawrence had saved several good shots, E. Taylor scored from a centre by Ashurst. • After this reverse the visitors showed some smart foot work, and caused trouble to Holdetn and Brinkswater to clear their lines. A free kick to the home team just outside the penalty area was well cleared by Lawrence, and then the visitors' for- wards transferred to the opposite end, and Gleave had to save a high shot from Derricott. The visitors were now playing the best game, and good shots by Derricott and Hughes were well saved by Gleave, and then Hughes hit the cross-bar with a fast drive. Taylor, with a fair opening, was a shade too wide, and then Robotham scored the visitors' fourth goal, and just before the close of play Collins added the fifth goal. Result: Brunswick Villa, 5 goals; San- key Athletic, 1 goal. ST. ELPHIN'S v. HICHFIELD JUNIORS. St. Elphin's opened their hyne fixtures on their new ground. Steel-street, with the Highfield Juniors as opponents. The following players turned out under the control of Mr. J. Wright::— St. Elphin's: Dorrian; Tunstall, Furness; Buck- ley, Miller, Watson; Chisholm, England,' A. Jones, Gleave and Holden. Highfield Juniors: Thornhill; Dunn, Jepson; Wishlade, Hankey, Wilding; Highfield, Moulsdale, Bell, Ford and Tomlinson. Both ends were quickly visited, and a very fast pace was set. After five minutes' play Ford gained possession and dribbled finely, beating 'several opponents and finally beat Dorrian with a high shot. The Saints followed with a strong attack, but England's final effort was erratic. Jones, the Saints' centre-forward, supplied Holden with some neat passes, and the latter trade the scores equal after fifteen minutes' play. After some midfield play the same player gained the lead for the Saints. Ford, the visitors' pivot, made many fine attempts to break through, but received poor support from his colleagues. Jones improved a grand centre by Chisholm, Thornhill being welt beaten for a third time. Ford was again prominent for the visitors, and it was only by sterling play that Furness pre- vented him scoring. During a scrimmage in front of the visitors' goal Holden scored again. Half- time: St. Elphin's, 4 goals; Highfield, 1 goal. The second portion opened very spiritedly, and the visitors attacked keenly, but failed to improve their position. England broke away and added a fifth for the Saints. From the centre kick Bell, when getting near the danger zone, was dispos- sessed by Furness, and England transferred with a grand run. and Jones. with a fine shot, added the sixth point. The inside forwards of the Saints were showing a good understanding, and Jones hit the cross-bar with a fast shot. England scoring from the rebound. The visitors played up very keenly in spite of their arrears, and Dorrian made a good save from a hard shot by Ford. Owing to their keenness the,visitors lost several good openings by infringing the off-side rule. Chisholm added to the Saints' total before the final whistle. Result: St. Elphin's; 7 goals; Highfield, 1 goal. For the winners, Furness played a very smart game at full-back, and Miller was the best of the halves. Jones was very smart at Centre-forward, Ad played unselfishly throughout, and Holden and the "hat trick" for the Saints. Thornhill, the 'visitors' goalkeeper, was not very reliable, and ought to have saved some of the shots that beat him. Ford and Bell were the outstanding for- wards. The tit-bit of to-day's fixtures is the meeting of the St. Elphin's and Howley United in Victoria Park. Both teams will be at full strength, and a very keen game is sure to be played- 'LEA IS FOR 'TO-DAY. EMMANUEL V. SANKEY ATELETIC. At Sankey. Emmanuel Team: H. Wright; S. Hughes and J. Hallam; W.
